# Chapter 4: Extreme Heat and Urban Health

*Theme: extreme heat, urban heat islands and the physiology of heat death*

Heat is the deadliest climate hazard in most years, and the least visible. A flood leaves debris, a fire leaves ash, a hurricane leaves a coastline that can be photographed from space. A heatwave leaves bodies in apartments, morgues that fill quietly, and death certificates that list cardiac arrest rather than exposure. The number of people killed by heat is therefore almost always discovered later, by epidemiologists comparing the deaths that occurred with the deaths that would have been expected, rather than by officials counting at the scene.

The physical basis is simple. The human body produces heat continuously and must lose it to the environment. It does so by radiation, convection, evaporation of sweat and, to a small extent, by breathing. When air temperature approaches skin temperature, radiation and convection stop working and evaporation becomes the only route. When humidity is also high, evaporation slows, and the body's core temperature begins to rise. At a wet-bulb temperature near 35°C — the theoretical limit at which sweat can no longer cool the body — even a healthy adult at rest will overheat within hours.

Cities make this worse in ways that are measurable. Concrete and asphalt absorb solar radiation during the day and release it at night, so the urban heat island is strongest after sunset, when the body most needs relief. Buildings block wind, traffic adds waste heat, and the loss of trees removes shade and transpiration. The result is that a heatwave of the same meteorological magnitude can be survivable in a village and lethal in a city, and that the difference is concentrated in the neighbourhoods with the least green cover and the oldest housing.

This chapter examines heat as a physical hazard, as a physiological stress, as an urban design problem and as a public health emergency. It pays particular attention to the question that determines whether a heatwave becomes a disaster: who is exposed, who is warned, and who has somewhere cooler to go.

## 4.1 Measuring Heat: Temperature, Humidity and Wet-Bulb

A thermometer in the shade measures air temperature, but the body responds to a combination of temperature, humidity, wind and radiation. The heat index, familiar from weather forecasts, approximates the apparent temperature by combining temperature and relative humidity. Wet-bulb temperature goes further: it is the lowest temperature to which a surface can be cooled by evaporation, and it is the variable most closely tied to the body's ability to shed heat. A dry 45°C is dangerous; a humid 35°C wet-bulb is close to unsurvivable for more than a few hours.

The theoretical limit of 35°C wet-bulb was proposed by Sherwood and Huber (2010) on the basis of a resting metabolic rate. Laboratory work since then has found that the critical wet-bulb temperature for healthy young adults at rest is closer to 31°C, and lower still during light activity (Vecellio, Wolf, Cottle and Kenney, 2022). Older people, people with cardiovascular disease, pregnant women and children reach their limits earlier. The practical implication is that conditions once considered rare are already occurring in South Asia and the Persian Gulf, and that the margin between discomfort and death is narrower than the 35°C figure suggests.

Heat is also cumulative. A single hot day is survivable; a week of hot nights is not, because sleep is when the body recovers. Public health agencies therefore increasingly report heat in terms of consecutive days above a threshold, or degree-days, rather than daily maxima. The distinction matters for warnings: a forecast of 40°C for one afternoon is a different emergency from a forecast of 35°C for seven consecutive days with nights above 28°C.

## 4.2 The Physiology of Heat Illness

Heat illness progresses along a continuum. Heat cramps are painful muscle spasms caused by salt loss; heat exhaustion produces weakness, dizziness, headache and nausea as blood pools in the skin and the heart struggles to maintain blood pressure; heatstroke occurs when the core temperature exceeds about 40°C and the body's thermoregulation fails. Heatstroke is a medical emergency with a mortality rate that can exceed 50 per cent if cooling is delayed, and it can leave survivors with permanent neurological damage.

The pathway to death is often cardiovascular rather than thermal. During a heatwave, the heart works harder to pump blood to the skin for cooling, while sweating reduces blood volume. People with narrowed coronary arteries, heart failure or kidney disease can die of a heart attack or kidney failure without ever reaching the temperature that defines heatstroke. This is why excess mortality during heatwaves is dominated by cardiovascular and respiratory causes, and why death certificates rarely mention heat.

Certain medications increase risk. Diuretics reduce blood volume; beta-blockers limit the heart's ability to increase output; anticholinergics reduce sweating; some antidepressants impair the perception of thirst. People who live alone may not notice the onset of symptoms, and people with dementia may not be able to seek help. The physiological and the social therefore combine: the same temperature that causes discomfort in a healthy adult can be lethal to an isolated elderly person on the top floor of a building without shade.

## 4.3 The Urban Heat Island

The urban heat island is one of the best-documented effects of human settlement. Cities are typically 3 to 7°C warmer than their rural surroundings during the day and can be more than 10°C warmer at night. The causes are well understood: dark surfaces absorb more solar radiation, buildings have a high thermal mass and release heat slowly, the geometry of streets traps radiation, vehicles and air-conditioning units release waste heat, and vegetation that would otherwise cool the air through transpiration is scarce.

The intensity varies enormously within a single city. Satellite measurements of land surface temperature in Jakarta, Delhi, Manila and São Paulo show differences of 5 to 10°C between neighbourhoods a few kilometres apart, with the hottest areas corresponding to dense informal settlements, industrial zones and bare ground, and the coolest to parks, tree-lined streets and water bodies. Because these differences track income, the urban heat island is also a map of inequality: the people with the least capacity to buy cooling live in the hottest places.

Urban form determines how the heat island will change. A city that grows through sprawl with dark roofs and wide asphalt roads will intensify it; a city that protects green space, plants street trees, uses reflective materials and preserves ventilation corridors can keep it in check even as the climate warms. The decisions are made in building codes, zoning rules and investment plans, which is why heat policy is inseparable from urban planning.

## 4.4 Who Dies in a Heatwave

The epidemiology of heat mortality is remarkably consistent across countries. The elderly account for the majority of deaths, particularly those over 75 and those living alone. People with pre-existing cardiovascular, respiratory, renal or psychiatric conditions are at elevated risk. Outdoor workers, construction labourers, agricultural workers and delivery riders are exposed to the highest temperatures for the longest periods. Homeless people, prisoners, refugees and people in informal settlements are exposed both to heat and to the absence of cooling.

The Chicago heatwave of July 1995 is the canonical study. More than 700 people died in five days, most of them in their homes, and the distribution was not explained by temperature alone. Eric Klinenberg's social autopsy found that neighbourhoods with similar demographics and similar temperatures had very different death rates, and that the difference was associated with social isolation, fear of crime, and the quality of the local social infrastructure — whether people knew their neighbours, whether they trusted the city, whether they were willing to leave their apartments (Klinenberg, 2002).

The European heatwave of August 2003 killed an estimated 70,000 people, with France alone accounting for nearly 15,000. The deaths were concentrated among the elderly, many of whom were left in apartments without air conditioning while their families were on holiday, and the crisis exposed the absence of a public health plan for heat. The 2022 European summer, by contrast, killed about 61,700 people despite a much better warning system, which suggests that warnings alone are insufficient when the underlying vulnerability remains (Ballester et al., 2023).

## 4.5 Counting the Dead: Excess Mortality

Heat deaths are counted by comparing the number of deaths observed during a heatwave with the number expected from historical averages for the same period. The difference is excess mortality. The method is robust to the misclassification of individual death certificates, because it does not require the certifier to recognise heat as a cause; it only requires that the total number of deaths be counted accurately. This is why epidemiological estimates of heat deaths are usually much higher than official counts.

The method has limitations. Heat can displace deaths that would have occurred within weeks — a phenomenon known as mortality displacement, or harvesting — which means that a heatwave may accelerate deaths rather than cause them. Studies generally find that displacement accounts for a minority of excess deaths, especially for severe heatwaves, but the size of the effect is contested. Excess mortality also captures deaths from other causes that rise during heatwaves, such as heart attacks and kidney failure, which may not be recorded as heat-related.

The global burden is large. The Lancet Countdown estimates that heat-related mortality among people over 65 has increased by about 70 per cent since the early 1990s, and that more than 500,000 deaths a year are associated with heat. The figure is an estimate, not a count, and it depends on assumptions about temperature-mortality relationships that vary by location. But the direction is unambiguous, and the trend is one of the clearest signals that climate change is already affecting human health.

## 4.6 Heat Action Plans and Early Warning

Ahmedabad, India, adopted one of the first heat action plans in South Asia after a 2010 heatwave killed more than 1,300 people. The plan combines a colour-coded forecast system, cooling centres in public buildings, adjusted working hours for outdoor labourers, water distribution, and outreach to slum communities through community health workers. An evaluation published in 2018 estimated that the plan had prevented about 1,190 deaths a year, though the authors cautioned that the estimate depended on assumptions about the counterfactual (Hess et al., 2018).

The components of an effective plan are now fairly standard. A warning system that triggers specific actions at specific thresholds; a communication strategy that reaches people in their own languages through channels they trust; a registry of vulnerable people, particularly the elderly living alone; designated cooling spaces that are open, accessible and free; and a protocol for hospitals and emergency services to prepare for surges. The weakest link is usually the last mile: the warning exists, but the person at risk does not hear it, cannot act on it, or has nowhere to go.

Plans also fail when they are designed for the wrong hazard. A plan built around daily maximum temperature will miss a heatwave with moderate daytime temperatures and very hot nights, which is precisely the pattern that kills in humid cities. Plans built around a single city will miss rural workers and informal settlements outside the administrative boundary. And plans that are not rehearsed will not be implemented when the emergency arrives, because the agencies involved will be learning their roles in real time.

## 4.7 Heat at Work: Productivity and Labour

Heat affects economies through labour as well as through death. The International Labour Organization estimates that heat stress already reduces global working hours by about 2.2 per cent, equivalent to 80 million full-time jobs, and that the loss could rise to 2.4 per cent by 2030 under a high-emission scenario. Agriculture accounts for about 60 per cent of the lost hours, followed by construction and industry. The losses are concentrated in South Asia, Sub-Saharan Africa and Southeast Asia, where outdoor work is common and cooling is scarce.

The mechanism is physiological. Above about 26°C wet-bulb, the body must divert blood to the skin to dissipate heat, which reduces the blood available to muscles and the brain. Physical capacity declines, error rates rise, and the risk of accidents increases. Workers adapt by taking breaks, shifting hours to early morning or night, and drinking more water, but these adaptations have limits, and they are often unavailable to workers paid by output rather than by time.

The economic consequence is not only lost output but lost income. A construction worker who cannot work during the hottest hours loses a fraction of daily wages; a farmer whose crop fails loses a season. Because informal workers rarely have heat insurance or sick leave, the cost is transferred to the household. Heat policy is therefore also labour policy, and the most effective interventions often involve collective bargaining, enforceable rest breaks and access to shade and water on site.

## 4.8 Cooling, Energy and Equity

Air conditioning saves lives, and its spread is one of the reasons heat mortality has fallen in high-income countries even as heatwaves have become more severe. The International Energy Agency counted about 1.6 billion air-conditioning units in 2016 and projected that the number could reach 5.6 billion by 2050, with most of the growth in developing countries. Cooling already accounts for about a tenth of global electricity consumption, and demand peaks during exactly the hours when the grid is most stressed.

The equity problem is that the people who most need cooling are least able to afford it. In many cities, electricity is unreliable, the upfront cost of an air conditioner is prohibitive, and landlords do not pay for efficiency improvements. Passive cooling — shade, insulation, reflective roofs, cross-ventilation, courtyards, trees — is cheaper and does not increase emissions, but it requires design decisions that are locked in for decades and is difficult to retrofit into informal housing.

There is also a feedback loop. Air conditioning releases waste heat into the street, raising the outdoor temperature and increasing the demand for cooling in neighbouring buildings. The effect is small relative to the heat island but measurable in dense districts. The solution is not to discourage cooling but to combine it with efficiency standards, building codes, urban greening and protection for people who cannot pay — a package that treats cooling as infrastructure rather than as a consumer good.

## 4.9 Designing Cooler Cities

Urban heat can be reduced by design, and the interventions are well understood. Street trees are the most effective single measure: a mature canopy can lower surface temperature by 10 to 15°C and air temperature by 2 to 4°C within its shade. Reflective roofs and pavements raise albedo and reduce heat absorption. Green roofs and walls insulate buildings and cool the air through transpiration. Water features, parks and ventilation corridors allow cool air to move through the city.

The evidence base has improved. A study of 601 European cities estimated that increasing tree cover to 30 per cent could have prevented about 2,600 heat deaths a year, roughly a third of the total, and that the benefit was largest in southern and eastern Europe (Iungman et al., 2023). Modelling of US cities found that reflective roofs and street trees could reduce afternoon temperatures by 1.5 to 3°C in dense neighbourhoods. The interventions pay for themselves through reduced cooling demand, improved air quality and lower mortality.

The obstacle is not knowledge but implementation. Trees take decades to mature, compete for space with utilities and parking, and require maintenance that municipal budgets often lack. Reflective roofs need building codes that are enforced. Ventilation corridors need planning rules that resist the pressure to build on every open space. The cities that succeed tend to have a dedicated heat office, a long-term budget and a political constituency that treats heat as a public health issue rather than an inconvenience.

## 4.10 The Limits of Adaptation

Adaptation to heat has real limits. Once wet-bulb temperatures approach the physiological threshold, no amount of shading, cooling or behaviour change can make outdoor work safe, and even indoor spaces without mechanical cooling become lethal. The regions most exposed — the Indus Valley, the Persian Gulf, the North China Plain, parts of West Africa — are also regions with large populations, limited air conditioning and fragile grids. In those places, adaptation buys time but does not remove the hazard.

The second limit is economic. Cooling for everyone would require a massive expansion of electricity generation, much of which would need to be low-carbon to avoid worsening the problem it addresses. The International Energy Agency estimates that efficient cooling could halve the growth in demand relative to a business-as-usual path, but efficiency requires standards, financing and enforcement that many countries lack. The third limit is behavioural: people underestimate their own vulnerability, especially if they have survived previous heatwaves, and warnings that are repeated without visible consequences lose credibility.

These limits do not make adaptation pointless. They define the boundary between what can be managed and what must be prevented. A city that plants trees, insulates homes, protects outdoor workers and opens cooling centres can prevent most heat deaths at 1.5°C or 2°C of warming. At 3°C or 4°C, the same city will face conditions that no amount of planning can fully offset. That is why heat policy must be paired with emission reduction: adaptation and mitigation are not alternatives but two halves of the same decision.

## 4.11 Case Study: The European Summer of 2022

The summer of 2022 was the hottest in Europe since records began, and it was preceded by a spring drought that had already lowered rivers and dried soils. Temperatures exceeded 40°C in Britain for the first time, reached 47°C in Portugal, and persisted across the Mediterranean for weeks. Wildfires burned more than 800,000 hectares across the continent, the largest area in the European record, and the combined effect of heat and drought reduced maize and sunflower harvests by double-digit percentages.

The mortality estimate published in Nature Medicine attributed about 61,700 deaths to the summer, with the highest numbers in Italy, Spain, Germany and France (Ballester et al., 2023). The figure was lower than the 70,000 of 2003 in absolute terms, but the summer was hotter, which suggests that adaptation had reduced the death rate per degree even as the absolute burden remained enormous. The authors noted that the estimate depended on the choice of temperature-mortality model and that the true figure could be higher.

The pattern of deaths revealed the limits of adaptation. Most deaths occurred among people over 75, many with cardiovascular or respiratory disease, and many in households without air conditioning. Southern European countries with older housing stock and less air conditioning had higher death rates than northern countries with cooler climates but better building standards. The lesson is that vulnerability is not simply a function of latitude: it is a function of housing, income, age structure and the presence of a plan.

The policy response varied. France, which had built a national heat plan after 2003, activated a colour-coded alert system, opened cooling centres and sent text messages to vulnerable households. Spain and Italy introduced restrictions on outdoor work during the hottest hours. Britain, which had no comparable plan, declared a national emergency for the first time and cancelled appointments at hospitals that were already under pressure. The differences in outcome are difficult to isolate because many factors changed at once, but the comparison suggests that plans reduce deaths when they are specific, funded and rehearsed.

The summer also exposed the interaction between heat and other systems. Railways buckled in Britain, nuclear plants in France reduced output because river water was too warm for cooling, and hospitals cancelled surgeries because operating theatres could not be kept cool. These failures were not caused by the temperature itself but by infrastructure designed for a cooler climate. They illustrate the central point of this chapter: heat becomes a disaster when it encounters systems that were not built to tolerate it.

For planners, the case suggests three priorities. The first is to treat heat as a health emergency with a dedicated budget, not as a weather inconvenience. The second is to identify the people at risk before the heatwave, through registries of elderly and isolated residents, rather than trying to find them during it. The third is to protect the infrastructure that heat disables — power, water, transport, hospitals — because a heatwave that shuts down the grid becomes a different and much larger emergency.

## 4.12 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Heat in a Warmer World

The historical record shows a clear trend. The number of days with extreme heat has increased on every continent, the frequency of heatwaves has risen by a factor of roughly three since the 1950s, and the hottest year on record has been exceeded repeatedly in the past decade. Heat mortality has risen with it: the Lancet Countdown estimates that heat deaths among people over 65 have increased by about 70 per cent since the 1990s, and that the trend is accelerating in Europe and South Asia.

Present conditions vary enormously by place. In Delhi, the summer of 2024 produced weeks of temperatures above 45°C and a record electricity demand; in Jakarta, the combination of high humidity and a strong urban heat island produces wet-bulb conditions that are already dangerous for outdoor workers; in Karachi, the 2015 heatwave killed more than 1,200 people in a city where many neighbourhoods lack reliable water and power. These are not future scenarios. They are the baseline from which the projections begin.

The assumptions that drive the projections are therefore about adaptation as much as about emissions. If cities invest in shade, cooling, warning systems and housing, mortality can fall even as temperatures rise. If they do not, mortality will rise faster than the temperature because exposure is growing with urbanisation and ageing. The scenarios below combine the two: they show what happens to heat risk under different emission paths and different levels of adaptive investment.

[[TABLE:Table 4.1 Heat exposure in selected cities, present and 2050]]
| City | Days above 35°C now | Days above 35°C in 2050 (SSP2-4.5) | Wet-bulb risk | Critical adaptation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Delhi | ~100 | ~140 | High in monsoon | Cooling centres; outdoor work bans |
| Karachi | ~60 | ~100 | Very high | Water supply; power reliability |
| Jakarta | ~30 | ~70 | Very high | Shade; drainage; social housing |
| Lagos | ~20 | ~60 | High | Grid; informal settlement upgrading |
| Athens | ~30 | ~55 | Moderate | Building codes; tree canopy |
| Phoenix | ~110 | ~150 | Low but extreme | Cooling for homeless; grid capacity |

Two conclusions follow. The first is that heat risk is not determined by temperature alone: the same number of hot days produces very different mortality depending on housing, water, power and social protection. The second is that the largest gains come from interventions that are boring and cheap — shade, water, warnings, check-ins on elderly neighbours — rather than from technologies that are expensive and unproven. A city that does the boring things well can save thousands of lives at 1.5°C and still save many at 2.5°C, though the margin narrows.

What would change these conclusions? A breakthrough in cheap, efficient cooling would reduce mortality in hot countries but would increase electricity demand and emissions unless it were paired with clean generation. A collapse of the power grid during a heatwave would turn a manageable event into a catastrophe, as happened in Texas in 2021 and in India in 2012. And a wet-bulb event above the physiological threshold would create a situation in which adaptation is impossible and only evacuation remains.

---

# Chapter 5: Water Scarcity and Drought

*Theme: water scarcity, drought and the politics of a finite resource*

In June 2019, the reservoirs that supply Chennai, a city of about nine million people, fell to one per cent of their capacity. The city government declared a water emergency, trucks delivered water to neighbourhoods that had none, and trains carried water from the Cauvery river more than three hundred kilometres to the city. Offices asked employees to work from home. Restaurants closed. The monsoon arrived in October, and within weeks the reservoirs were full again — but the crisis had lasted four months, and it had been predicted for more than a decade.

Chennai is not an arid city. It receives about 1,400 millimetres of rain a year, more than London or Paris. The crisis was not caused by a lack of rainfall in the long term but by a sequence of weak monsoons, the loss of lakes and wetlands to construction, the depletion of groundwater, and a supply system that had not kept pace with growth. The city had built its prosperity on the assumption that water would always be available somewhere nearby, and the assumption failed.

The same assumption is failing across much of the world. Fresh water is a small fraction of the planet's water — about 2.5 per cent — and most of that is locked in glaciers or buried deep underground. The water that is accessible, in rivers, lakes and shallow aquifers, is renewed by rainfall and snowmelt at a rate that is essentially fixed. Humanity uses about half of that renewable supply, and in many regions it uses more, mining groundwater that took thousands of years to accumulate.

This chapter examines water scarcity as a physical, economic and political problem. It distinguishes between drought, which is a temporary shortage of rain, and scarcity, which is a persistent imbalance between demand and supply. It examines the agricultural, urban and industrial demands that drive the imbalance, and the adaptations — storage, efficiency, reuse, desalination, pricing and institutions — that can restore it. It closes with the question that will shape the next century: what happens when a shared river no longer carries enough water for the people who depend on it.

## 5.1 The Physics and Geography of Fresh Water

The global water cycle moves about 500,000 cubic kilometres of water through evaporation and precipitation each year, but only about 40,000 cubic kilometres falls on land, and only a fraction of that is accessible to humans at a reasonable cost. Rivers and lakes hold about 90,000 cubic kilometres; groundwater holds about 10 million cubic kilometres, but most of it is deep, saline or unrenewable. The renewable freshwater supply that humanity can use is therefore a thin and unevenly distributed layer of the planet's water.

Distribution is as important as quantity. Nine countries hold about half of the world's renewable freshwater, while forty countries have less than 1,000 cubic metres per person per year — the threshold commonly used to define water scarcity. The Middle East and North Africa are the most water-scarce regions, but scarcity is also severe in parts of India, Pakistan, Mexico and South Africa, where growing populations and economies have outgrown the supply that rainfall provides.

Climate change alters the cycle in three ways. It increases evaporation as temperatures rise, which reduces the water available in soils and reservoirs. It changes precipitation patterns, making rain more concentrated in intense events and less reliable as a steady supply. And it reduces snowpack and glacier storage, which acts as a natural reservoir that releases water slowly through the dry season. The third effect is especially important in the Himalayas, the Andes and the Alps, where millions of people depend on meltwater.

These physical facts do not by themselves produce scarcity. Scarcity is produced by the interaction between supply and the institutions that allocate it. A country can have abundant water and still experience shortages if its infrastructure leaks, its agriculture is inefficient, its cities grow without planning, or its water rights are allocated to uses that no longer make sense. Understanding that distinction is the first step toward treating scarcity as a problem that can be governed rather than as a fate that must be endured.

## 5.2 Drought: Four Definitions

Drought is usually defined in four ways. Meteorological drought is a prolonged period of below-average rainfall. Agricultural drought is a shortage of soil moisture during the growing season, which can occur even when rainfall is normal if the rain falls at the wrong time or runs off compacted ground. Hydrological drought is a depletion of rivers, reservoirs and groundwater, which usually develops more slowly than the other two. Socioeconomic drought occurs when the shortage begins to affect the supply of goods and services, including food, energy and water.

The four definitions explain why drought is often declared late. A government may see normal rainfall and conclude that there is no drought, while farmers are already losing a season because the rain came in one week instead of eight. Hydrological drought may persist for years after the rain returns, because aquifers and reservoirs recover slowly. The 2022 Horn of Africa drought was declared after five consecutive failed rainy seasons, by which time more than twenty million people were acutely food insecure.

Drought also has a long tail. The economic losses extend beyond the harvest: livestock die, rural incomes collapse, migration accelerates, and children drop out of school. A study of the 2011–2017 drought in the Horn of Africa found that the effects on nutrition and education persisted for years after the rains returned. Drought is therefore not an event that ends when the rain begins; it is a shock whose consequences are transmitted through households and institutions.

## 5.3 Groundwater: The Invisible Reserve

Groundwater is the largest accessible store of fresh water on Earth and the source of drinking water for about half the world's population. It is also the most vulnerable to overuse, because it is invisible: a farmer who pumps a well cannot see the aquifer declining, and a city that licenses new wells cannot easily measure the cumulative effect. The result is a global pattern of depletion that has been called a slow-moving crisis.

The Ogallala aquifer under the US Great Plains is the canonical example. It supplies water to about 30 per cent of US irrigated land, and parts of it have lost more than half their saturated thickness. In some areas of Kansas and Texas, the water table has fallen by more than 50 metres, and farmers have abandoned irrigation. The aquifer recharges at a rate of a few millimetres a year, while withdrawals are measured in metres, which means that the resource is being mined rather than used.

Similar patterns appear in the Indo-Gangetic Plain, the North China Plain, Mexico's central plateau, and the Arabian Peninsula. In Jakarta, groundwater extraction has caused land subsidence of up to 25 centimetres a year in some districts, which increases flood risk and has contributed to the decision to move the national capital. The common feature is that the water is being used faster than it is replenished, and that the users have no direct incentive to stop because the cost of depletion falls on someone else, often on future generations.

There are success stories. Arizona's Groundwater Management Act of 1980 required new developments to demonstrate a hundred-year water supply, and it slowed depletion in the most populated areas, though it did not eliminate it. Israel's water economy combines drip irrigation, recycled wastewater and desalination to support agriculture and a growing population with less water per capita than any other developed country. The lesson is that groundwater can be governed, but only with measurement, enforcement and a legal framework that treats the aquifer as a shared resource.

## 5.4 Rivers Under Stress

The great rivers of the world are the clearest visible measure of water stress. The Colorado River no longer reaches the sea in most years; its flow has declined by about 20 per cent since the early twentieth century, and the two largest reservoirs, Lake Mead and Lake Powell, fell below 30 per cent of capacity in 2022. The river supplies water to about 40 million people and 1.2 million hectares of farmland across seven US states and northern Mexico, under an allocation system designed in 1922 that assumed a flow larger than the river has delivered.

The Yellow River in China has been fully allocated since the 1990s and sometimes fails to reach the sea; the Indus supplies a basin that is home to more than 250 million people and is dependent on snow and glacier melt that is projected to decline; the Murray–Darling in Australia has experienced severe droughts and ecosystem collapse in its lower reaches. In each case, the river is both a water supply and an ecosystem, and the two uses compete for the same flow.

The politics of rivers are upstream-downstream politics. A dam built upstream reduces the flow available downstream; a city that withdraws water for its residents reduces the water available for farmers; a country that claims a share based on historical use may be claiming water that other countries believe is theirs. International river basins cover about half the land surface of the planet and are home to about 40 per cent of its population, which is why water is often described as a source of both cooperation and conflict.

## 5.5 Water Quality and the Hidden Scarcity

Water can be physically present and still unusable. Contamination by pathogens, nitrogen and phosphorus from agriculture, heavy metals, arsenic and industrial chemicals reduces the supply of safe water as effectively as a drought. The World Health Organization estimates that about 2.2 billion people lack access to safely managed drinking water, and that contaminated water is associated with hundreds of thousands of diarrhoeal deaths a year, most of them among children under five.

Arsenic is a particularly insidious example. Wells drilled in Bangladesh and West Bengal in the 1970s to provide microbiologically safe water inadvertently tapped aquifers contaminated with naturally occurring arsenic. Tens of millions of people were exposed, and the health consequences — skin lesions, cancers, cardiovascular disease — appeared decades later. The episode is a reminder that a technological solution can create a new problem while solving an old one, and that water quality must be monitored as carefully as water quantity.

Salinity is another hidden form of scarcity. In coastal aquifers, over-extraction draws seawater inland; in irrigated regions, rising water tables bring salts to the surface, where evaporation concentrates them. Salinisation has degraded hundreds of millions of hectares of farmland and continues to spread in the Indus basin, the Tigris–Euphrates plain and parts of Australia. Unlike a drought, salinity does not end when the rain returns; it can make land unusable for decades.

## 5.6 Agriculture: The Largest User

Agriculture accounts for about 70 per cent of global freshwater withdrawals, and in some developing countries the share is closer to 90 per cent. Irrigation underpins about 20 per cent of cultivated land but produces about 40 per cent of global food, which means that any constraint on irrigation has an outsized effect on food security. The efficiency of that use is often low: flood irrigation loses large amounts to evaporation and runoff, and poorly maintained canals leak before the water reaches the field.

The gains from improving efficiency are large but not automatic. Drip irrigation can reduce water use by 30 to 50 per cent compared with flood irrigation while maintaining or increasing yields, but it requires capital, maintenance and a reliable supply of clean water. In India, the world's largest user of groundwater, subsidised electricity for pumping has encouraged over-extraction, and the response has been a mix of subsidies for drip systems, restrictions on new wells and, in some states, direct payments to farmers who switch to less water-intensive crops.

The deeper problem is that water is usually priced below its cost. A farmer who pays nothing for water has no incentive to economise, and a city that charges less than the cost of supply cannot maintain its pipes. Pricing reform is politically difficult because water is a basic need and because farmers are organised. The alternatives — quotas, tradable rights, tiered tariffs that protect basic consumption while charging more for luxury use — are all attempts to impose a price signal without denying people the water they need to survive.

## 5.7 Urban Water Systems

Cities concentrate demand and lose water before it reaches the tap. Non-revenue water — the difference between what a utility produces and what it bills — averages about 30 per cent globally and exceeds 50 per cent in some cities. The losses come from leaking pipes, illegal connections, metering errors and unbilled public use. In a city with a shortage, repairing the distribution network can be cheaper than building a new reservoir.

Urban growth also consumes the landscapes that once stored water. Chennai's lakes and wetlands were built over as the city expanded; Jakarta's groundwater was pumped because the piped network did not reach most neighbourhoods; Mexico City's aquifers were drained faster than they could recharge. The result is that the city becomes dependent on distant sources, which increases cost, vulnerability and political tension with the regions that supply it.

The responses that work combine supply, demand and storage. Singapore's water strategy — local catchment, imported water, desalination and recycled water, known as the four national taps — is the most cited example, and it has allowed a city-state with no natural aquifer to achieve water security. Windhoek, Namibia, has been recycling wastewater directly into the drinking supply since 1968. These cases show that scarcity is not simply a function of rainfall; it is a function of how deliberately a city manages the cycle.

## 5.8 Drought, Food and Prices

Drought transmits to the economy through food prices. When a major producing region loses a harvest, global prices rise, and the effect is largest for poor households that spend a large share of their income on food. The 2010–2011 drought in Russia and the subsequent export ban contributed to the global food price spike that year; the 2022 drought in Europe reduced maize and sunflower output and added to the price pressures already caused by the war in Ukraine.

The transmission is not automatic, because trade, storage and substitution can absorb part of the shock. But those buffers are thinner than they appear. Global grain stocks are held by a small number of countries, and exporters sometimes restrict shipments in a crisis, which amplifies the price increase. Importing countries respond by buying more, which amplifies it further. The result is that a regional drought can become a global price event through the interaction of policy responses rather than through the physical shortage alone.

For households, the consequence is a change in diet. When prices rise, families substitute cheaper staples, reduce protein and fresh produce, and sometimes skip meals. The nutritional effect persists long after prices fall, particularly for children, whose growth and cognitive development can be permanently affected. Drought is therefore a food security problem as much as a water problem, and the two cannot be addressed separately.

## 5.9 Water Conflict and Cooperation

Water has been called a source of war, but the historical record is more complex. The Pacific Institute's Water Conflict Chronology records thousands of water-related tensions, most of them local, and far more instances of cooperation than of armed conflict over water. The Nile, the Indus, the Mekong and the Rhine all have long histories of negotiation, and some of the most durable treaties in the world are water treaties.

That does not mean water is not a source of conflict. It means that the conflict usually takes the form of displacement, dispossession and violence within countries rather than war between them. Dam construction displaces communities; irrigation withdrawals deprive downstream farmers; pollution destroys fisheries; water tariffs provoke protests. The risk of interstate war over water is real but rare, and it is highest where water scarcity interacts with existing political hostility and weak institutions.

The cooperative record offers lessons. The Indus Waters Treaty of 1960 survived three wars between India and Pakistan and has been remarkably resilient, because it created a joint commission with technical authority and a dispute-resolution mechanism. The Nile Basin Initiative, the Mekong River Commission and the Senegal River Organisation show that shared basins can be governed when the parties have an interest in the information and the rules that the institution provides. Water is more often a reason to talk than a reason to fight.

## 5.10 Adaptation: Storage, Efficiency and Reuse

The portfolio of water adaptations is well known. Storage — dams, reservoirs, aquifer recharge, rainwater harvesting — bridges the gap between wet and dry seasons. Efficiency — drip irrigation, leak repair, industrial recycling — produces more output per cubic metre. Reuse — treating wastewater for irrigation or industry — turns a waste stream into a resource. Desalination — removing salt from seawater or brackish groundwater — creates new supply at a cost in energy and money.

Each option has limits. Dams displace people and silt up; the world has built most of the good sites. Efficiency can be offset by rebound effects, in which farmers use the saved water to expand irrigated area. Desalination is expensive, energy-intensive and produces brine that must be disposed of carefully; it is affordable for wealthy coastal cities but not for inland agriculture. Reuse requires treatment, monitoring and public acceptance, which is often the hardest part.

The most important adaptation is institutional. Water is managed through rights, prices, quotas and basin agreements, and the difference between a well-managed basin and a poorly managed one is often larger than the difference between a wet year and a dry one. Australia's Murray–Darling Basin Plan, which caps total withdrawals and allows water to be traded, is an example of an attempt to manage a basin as a system rather than as a collection of individual claims. It has been criticised for the environmental outcomes it has not delivered, but it demonstrates that the institutional design is at least as important as the engineering.

## 5.11 Case Study: Chennai's Day Zero

Chennai's water crisis did not arrive without warning. The city depends on four reservoirs and on groundwater, and the reservoirs had been declining for years as urban expansion covered the lakes that once captured monsoon rain. In 2018 the northeast monsoon failed, and by June 2019 the reservoirs held about one per cent of their capacity. The city government declared a water emergency and began to truck water to neighbourhoods, while the state arranged for trains to carry water from the Cauvery.

The crisis affected different residents differently. Households with private wells and storage tanks managed; households in informal settlements, which depended on public taps and tanker deliveries, queued for hours. Schools closed, restaurants reduced service, and information technology companies asked staff to work from home. The economic cost was estimated in the hundreds of millions of dollars, and the political cost was higher: the crisis became a symbol of the failure of urban water management in a rapidly growing city.

The response included short-term measures — tankers, trains, restrictions on non-essential use — and longer-term investments in desalination, sewage treatment and rainwater harvesting. The city also began to restore some of the lakes and wetlands that had been built over, recognising that the cheapest storage was the landscape itself. By 2024, the reservoirs had recovered, but the underlying imbalance had not been resolved: the city still depends on a monsoon that is becoming less reliable and on groundwater that is being depleted.

The case illustrates a pattern that appears in cities around the world. Water crises are rarely caused by a single year of low rainfall; they are caused by the accumulation of decisions that reduced storage, increased demand and deferred maintenance. The crisis is the moment when the accumulated deficit becomes visible. Because the deficit is institutional, the solution must be institutional: a plan that combines supply, demand, storage and governance, and that is implemented before the reservoirs empty.

For other cities, the lessons are specific. First, measure and publicly report the water balance, including losses in the distribution network and the rate of groundwater depletion. Second, protect the landscapes that store water — lakes, wetlands, floodplains and recharge areas — before they are built over. Third, diversify supply, including reuse and desalination where affordable, and price water in a way that funds maintenance. Fourth, identify the households that will be hit first and build a delivery system that reaches them, because the crisis will expose the difference between a city that has water and a city that has access to it.

## 5.12 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Water in 2050

The historical data show a consistent trend. Global water withdrawals have roughly tripled since 1950, driven by population growth, irrigation and industrial demand. Groundwater depletion has accelerated in the past two decades, and the area of land affected by drought has increased. The World Bank estimates that water scarcity could reduce GDP growth in some regions by up to 6 per cent by 2050, and the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification estimates that drought could displace 700 million people by 2030.

Present conditions give the baseline. About 4 billion people experience severe water scarcity for at least one month a year, and 2.2 billion lack safely managed drinking water. Agriculture uses about 70 per cent of withdrawals, and in many basins the allocation of water exceeds the renewable supply. The gap is filled by groundwater mining, which cannot continue indefinitely. The question is not whether the deficit will be closed but how: by efficiency, by reallocation, by new supply, or by crisis.

The assumptions that drive the projections are population, diet, technology and governance. A world that converges toward efficient irrigation, recycles more water and limits groundwater withdrawal can meet demand with less water than it uses today. A world that continues current trends will face a shortfall of about 40 per cent between available supply and projected demand by 2030, according to the 2030 Water Resources Group. The scenarios below show what the main basins might look like under different assumptions.

[[TABLE:Table 5.1 Water stress in major basins, present and 2050]]
| Basin | Population (million) | Current stress | 2050 gap (SSP2-4.5) | Main adaptation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Indus | ~300 | Extreme | 30–40% | Drip irrigation; groundwater regulation |
| Colorado | ~40 | Extreme | 20–30% | Demand caps; reuse; fallowing |
| Nile | ~280 | High | 25–35% | Storage; efficiency; basin agreement |
| Citarum (Java) | ~25 | Very high | 20–30% | Wastewater treatment; reforestation |
| Murray–Darling | ~2.4 | High | 10–20% | Water trading; environmental flows |
| Tigris–Euphrates | ~60 | Very high | 30–40% | Dam coordination; irrigation reform |

Two conclusions follow. The first is that the gap is largest in basins where agriculture dominates and groundwater is unregulated, which means that the most important interventions are agricultural rather than urban. The second is that the gap can be closed with existing technology; the obstacle is the political cost of reallocating water from one user to another. A basin that caps withdrawals will produce less in the short term, and the farmers who lose water will resist.

What would change these conclusions? A breakthrough in cheap desalination would expand supply for coastal cities but not for inland agriculture. A change in diet toward less water-intensive food would reduce demand substantially, since meat and dairy require far more water per calorie than grains. And a sustained increase in precipitation in some basins, which some climate projections suggest, would relieve pressure in those places — but the same projections show declines in others, and the uncertainty is largest where the stakes are highest.

---

# Chapter 6: Floods and Sea Level Rise

*Theme: floods and sea level rise*

The subject of this chapter is floods and sea level rise. The argument proceeds in three steps: what is happening, how we know, and what follows for decisions. Sources are cited as they are used, and contested points are marked as contested rather than presented as settled.

## 6.1 Flood Types and Causes

As Scott Kulp &  Benjamin Strauss (2019) argues, three times more people live below future high tide than old models showed — coastal risk was underestimated.

Kulp and Strauss corrected coastal elevation data using radar and found that far more land and people sit below future high-tide lines than earlier models showed. Their estimates made exposure visible at the scale of individual settlements. The projections assume stated emissions pathways. For flood types and causes, the bearing is direct: the constraint is institutional as much as physical.

Institutional performance in flood types and causes depends less on formal authority than on whether mandates, money and information arrive together. Mandates split across agencies produce the familiar gap between warning and response; budgets tied to calendar years produce the gap between relief and recovery (UNDRR, 2015). Neither gap requires bad faith, only an ordinary division of labour. The strain is already visible in the numbers: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). In the work of coastal planning and flood defence, the design question is where to place the authority that can move money and people within the same week. Coordination is not a virtue to be requested; it is a structure to be built.

Most disagreements about flood types and causes are not disagreements about facts. They are disagreements about weight: which risks count, over what horizon, and for whom (IPCC, 2022). That is why the same evidence can support both alarm and complacency without either side misreading the data. The scale involved is not marginal: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). For coastal planning and flood defence, clarity about the weighting matters as much as clarity about the measurement. Stating the criterion in advance is what separates analysis from advocacy.

The evidence base behind flood types and causes is larger than its public profile suggests, and more uneven. Satellite records, station data, insurance claims and household surveys answer different questions, and they rarely agree on the same decimal place (IPCC, 2021). Where they disagree, the disagreement is usually about coverage rather than about the direction of change. Confidence is highest on aggregates and lowest on localities: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). Within coastal planning and flood defence, the sensible use of the record is to establish direction and then test local plausibility rather than to import a global average wholesale. A number that cannot be traced to a source is not evidence; it is decoration.

Why things behave as they do is a question with a traceable answer. The chain runs from physical forcing through changing conditions to the decisions that place people and assets in harm's way (IPCC, 2021). Each link is observable, and that is what allows the pattern to be projected even where individual outcomes cannot be. The magnitudes involved are not small: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, there is more than one point of action, and the cheapest interventions are usually the earliest. Waiting for certainty at the end of the chain is the most expensive option available.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Typhoon Doksuri flooded Beijing in July 2023 with heaviest rain in 140 years, 1 million displaced (China CMA, Reuters). It is one line in a longer ledger, and the ledger is what matters. In the work of coastal planning and flood defence, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

Certainty is unevenly distributed across this literature. Detection is firm, projection is conditional, and cost estimates are contingent on choices not yet made. A reader who wants a single number will be disappointed; a reader who wants a defensible decision has what is needed. In the work of coastal planning and flood defence, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 6.2 Sea Level Rise Mechanisms

As Michael Oppenheimer (2020) argues, there is no scenario that stops sea level rise in this century — sea level is committed to rise for centuries due to ocean heat and ice-sheet response.

Oppenheimer's statement draws on his role as Lead Author of IPCC SROCC Chapter 4 (2019) on sea-level rise and coastal implications. 'No scenario stops sea level rise in this century' means even with strong mitigation, ocean thermal expansion and ice-sheet response continue due to system inertia. 'Committed to rise for centuries' indicates that sea level is a long-term commitment, with multi-meter potential depending on emissions, as grounded in paleoclimate and process models. The rate of rise — roughly doubled since the 1990s — already increases coastal flood frequency, even without stronger storms. Oppenheimer warned that planning based on historical flood maps underestimates future exposure for islands, deltas, and low-lying cities. For sea level rise mechanisms within floods and sea level rise, the analysis must shift from preventing rise to managing inevitable rise through elevation, protection, accommodation, and managed retreat, with timelines spanning decades to centuries. Read against sea level rise mechanisms, the point is that intention without capacity changes little.

Discussion of sea level rise mechanisms usually begins as a technical question, and that framing hides the decisions underneath it. The technical part is real: measurement, modelling and attribution have improved sharply over two decades (IPCC, 2023). But the contested part is rarely the measurement; it is what the numbers imply for who acts, who pays and who is protected. One figure sets the scale: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). Read that way, the question for coastal planning and flood defence is not whether the trend is real but which choices remain defensible across the plausible range. A framing that treats uncertainty as a reason to wait gets the logic backwards: uncertainty is what makes early, reversible decisions valuable. For coastal planning and flood defence, that is the difference between a plan that can be defended and one that merely exists.

The evidence base for sea level rise mechanisms is broad but uneven, and the unevenness is informative. Where many independent studies agree, confidence is high; where a single model family supplies the estimate, it is not (IPCC, 2021). One figure illustrates the state of knowledge: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). The appropriate response to a strong signal with weak local detail is not to wait for precision but to design for the range. Within coastal planning and flood defence, that means carrying uncertainty into the decision rather than resolving it beforehand. Plans that require certainty before acting are plans that act late.

The causal path can be traced step by step, which is why the broad direction is predictable even when individual events are not. A forcing alters conditions; conditions alter exposure; exposure meets a population with unequal capacity to absorb it (IPCC, 2022). Because the steps interact, impacts rarely arrive one at a time: heat comes with humidity, drought with fire risk, rainfall with runoff. The same pattern is visible in the numbers: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, the practical implication is to plan for combinations rather than single hazards. Systems designed for one stress at a time fail first when two arrive together.

Institutional capacity is what turns a warning into an evacuation and a budget line into a working shelter. It depends on mandates that are clear, data that are shared, and money that can be released without a new decision (UNDRR, 2015). Where those conditions are absent, the same hazard produces a larger disaster. The stakes are visible in the numbers: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). In the work of coastal planning and flood defence, capability is better tested in a drill than in a document. A plan that has never been exercised is a hypothesis.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: Peru's Cyclone Yaku (Mar 2023) brought unprecedented rains to arid north, 70 deaths (SENAMHI, Reuters). Set beside the record, the episode indicates direction without fixing the rate. For coastal planning and flood defence, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

What emerges is less a prediction than a test. A measure is adequate if it still works when the upper end of the range arrives; a plan is credible if it names who acts, with what authority and with what money. Judged that way, the gap between what is known and what is done is not a knowledge gap. For coastal planning and flood defence, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 6.3 Coastal Flood Risk

W. Neil Adger (2006) puts it plainly: vulnerability is not only exposure to hazards but the social architecture of entitlements, power, and capacity that determines who adapts.

Adger reframes vulnerability as a property of social arrangements rather than of hazards. Entitlements, power and access to resources decide who can prepare, who can leave and who recovers. The point is structural, not semantic. The implication for coastal flood risk is a question of sequence: what has to be in place before the event, not after it.

The record on coastal flood risk is now strong enough to be treated as a trend rather than an anomaly. The record is uneven — some regions and seasons move sharply, others barely — but the aggregate direction is consistent across methods (IPCC, 2023). The headline figure is blunt: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). What remains contested is attribution of individual events and the speed of future change, not whether the baseline has shifted. That distinction shapes how coastal planning and flood defence should read the numbers: as a distribution to plan against, not a forecast to await. Where the evidence is thin, the honest response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence. Within coastal planning and flood defence, the test is whether the arrangement survives contact with a bad week.

Following the sequence from cause to harm makes the points of intervention visible, and there are more of them than the public debate suggests. Emissions determine the forcing; land use and insurance determine exposure; income, health and warning determine capacity; each link can be altered by policy at a different scale (UNDRR, 2015). Because the links are separate, a failure at one does not excuse inaction at another. The scale at which the chain operates is large: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, the practical value of thinking in a chain is that it shows which link is cheapest to move this year. Intervention points are political choices, not physical facts.

Governance is tested at the seams between organisations. Most failures reported after disasters are failures of handover: warnings that never reached the right desk, plans that assumed capacity another agency had already committed (UNDRR, 2015). The evidence here is thinner than in the physical sciences, and should be described as such. One indication of the stakes: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). What the record does show is that authority, money and information have to move together; any one without the others stalls. In the work of coastal planning and flood defence, the test is whether a decision can be taken where the consequence is felt, with the resources to act on it.

How a problem is framed decides what counts as a solution, and coastal flood risk is a case in point. Framed as an environmental issue, it invites a ministry and a budget line; framed as a development issue, it invites land use, housing and finance at once (IPCC, 2022). The second framing is harder to administer precisely because it cannot be assigned to a single department. The scale involved rules out a narrow frame: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). For coastal planning and flood defence, the practical consequence is that the frame has to be chosen before the plan, not after it. Frames are cheap to change on paper and expensive to change in an institution.

A recent marker points the same way: Typhoon Doksuri flooded Beijing in July 2023 with heaviest rain in 140 years, 1 million displaced (China CMA, Reuters). Read against the longer record, it is an illustration rather than a proof. Within coastal planning and flood defence, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

Readers should know where this section stands on the evidence ladder. The direction of change is established; the pace is estimated; the local consequences are modelled. Statements that blur those levels are the ones most likely to mislead, and they are also the easiest to check. Within coastal planning and flood defence, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 6.4 Riverine and Flash Floods

As James Hansen (2013) argues, sea level rise of several meters could be expected with 2°C warming — 2°C would have consequences that can be described as disastrous.

Hansen's statement originates from his 2016 paper 'Ice melt, sea level rise and superstorms' and a 2013 op-ed that warned about the long-term consequences of 2°C warming. The phrase 'several meters' refers to paleoclimate evidence where past periods about 1-2°C warmer than preindustrial left sea levels 6-9 meters higher. The mechanism is not a slow linear creep but non-linear disintegration of the West Antarctic and Greenland ice sheets, amplified by ocean warming from below. The threshold of 2°C matters because it is widely presented as the upper Paris Agreement limit, often implying relative safety. Hansen argues this framing underestimates committed, century-scale risks that continue long after emissions peak. The word 'disastrous' captures his view that multi-meter rise would inundate major coastal cities, deltas, and displace hundreds of millions, with cascading economic and security effects. For riverine and flash floods within floods and sea level rise, the implication is that near-term temperature targets must be judged against long-term, irreversible sea-level commitments, not just mid-century warming levels. In the terms of riverine and flash floods, the claim shifts attention from prediction to preparation.

Impacts do not follow directly from a change in the average; they follow from the interaction between a shifting hazard and a fixed exposure. A house built to a hundred-year flood standard meets a twenty-five-year event with alarming regularity once the distribution has moved (IPCC, 2022). Nothing about the house changed; the standard it was designed against did. The shift is quantifiable: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, the lesson is that design standards have a shelf life and need a review date attached to them. An unrevised standard is a decision to accept a higher level of risk than the one originally debated.

Whether the problem remains manageable depends as much on institutions as on the underlying trend. Early warning, land-use rules, standards and social protection reduce harm, but only where authority and funding reach the level at which risk is felt (UNDRR, 2015). The common failure is fragmentation: overlapping mandates, unshared data, responsibility falling between agencies. Coordination is a design problem with measurable features — joint protocols, common baselines, clear triggers. In the work of coastal planning and flood defence, the test is whether its own arrangements have those features before the next event rather than after it. Where such arrangements exist, response times fall and losses follow.

It helps to ask what question riverine and flash floods is really answering. Framed as a measurement problem, it invites better instruments; framed as a decision problem, it invites better institutions (IPCC, 2023). The difference is practical, because the second framing admits trade-offs that the first hides. The scale is already visible: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021). For coastal planning and flood defence, the choice of framing determines which options are considered at all. A question that excludes trade-offs will produce an answer that cannot be implemented.

Evidence on riverine and flash floods rests on several independent lines rather than a single study. Satellites, ground stations, ocean measurements and model reanalyses now agree on direction, and disagree mainly on pace and regional detail (IPCC, 2021). One measure puts the magnitude in view: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Confidence is highest where those lines converge and lowest where one method or a short record carries the claim. Within coastal planning and flood defence, that means planning against a range rather than a point. A decision built on the central estimate alone is a decision that will be surprised.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Peru's Cyclone Yaku (Mar 2023) brought unprecedented rains to arid north, 70 deaths (SENAMHI, Reuters). The value of such reports is that they date the change rather than explain it. As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

What the section establishes is narrower than a prediction and firmer than an opinion. It establishes that the direction is known, that the costs of inaction are concentrated on those least able to bear them, and that the available measures are known well enough to be tested. As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 6.5 Case Study: Floods and Sea Level Rise

The case that anchors this chapter is documented and recent: Typhoon Doksuri flooded Beijing in July 2023 with heaviest rain in 140 years, 1 million displaced (China CMA, Reuters). What matters is not the event alone but the sequence it exposes — a hazard that exceeded design assumptions, a response constrained by mandate and money, and a recovery that revealed who was covered and who was not. As far as coastal planning and flood defence is concerned, the record supplies both the warning and the timetable.

Read as evidence rather than anecdote, the episode shows how a physical shock travels through housing, work, health and finance. Damage does not stop where the water recedes; it continues through lost income, interrupted schooling and depleted savings — a sequence coastal planning and flood defence can measure in its own records.

The test of any lesson drawn from the case is whether it changes a budget line, a standard or a protocol. If it changes none of the three, it remains a story.



## 6.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Floods and Sea Level Rise

For floods and sea-level rise, the near term rewrites return periods: by 2040 a 1-in-100-year flood becomes 1-in-20 in Jakarta Bay, the Mekong and Mississippi deltas, levees are overtopped 2–3 times more often, and tidal flooding reaches 30–50 days a year in zones below 2 m.

By 2050, 0.2–0.3 m of sea-level rise combines with heavier rainfall to make compound floods routine, and by 2100 a 0.6–1.0 m rise permanently claims 10–15% of coastal farmland and forces retreat from the lowest neighborhoods (IPCC, 2021; Oppenheimer et al., 2019).

Elevated roads and houses, mangrove belts 500 m wide, polders with reliable pumps, and buy-out or relocation offers for zones below 1 m, paired with livelihood support.

[[TABLE:Table 6.1 Future scenarios and predictions for floods and sea level rise (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | 1-in-100 → 1-in-30 flood; +0.10 m sea |
| 2050 | 1-in-100 → 1-in-15; 20–30 tidal days |
| 2100 | 0.6–1.0 m; 10–15% coastal farmland lost |

Watch annual tidal-flood days, the highest monthly tide against your street level, and drainage overflow after ordinary storms; sea level is high-confidence, but local flooding depends on subsidence and pumps (IPCC, 2022).
