# Chapter 7: Food Security and Agriculture

*Theme: food security and agriculture under climate change*

This chapter deals with food security and agriculture under climate change. Each section opens with a claim, tests it against the evidence and draws out the implication for the people who have to act on it. The aim is not completeness but a defensible line through a large literature.

## 7.1 Food Systems Overview

As David Lobell (2011) argues, warming since 1980 has already cut wheat and maize yields, masking gains from technology.

Lobell's analysis separated climate effects from technology and management in global crop records. Warming since 1980 reduced wheat and maize yields relative to a counterfactual without it. Adaptation and carbon fertilisation offset part, but not all, of the loss. Applied to food systems overview, the argument sets a test: does the measure still work when capacity is stretched?

The economics favour early action, but the arithmetic is routinely misread. Damage functions show rising marginal losses with warming, while preventive spending delivers returns over decades (World Bank, 2022). The magnitudes are large: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). The obstacle is less the total than the distribution of costs and benefits: those who pay are rarely those who are protected. As far as food systems and rural livelihoods is concerned, the implication is to count avoided losses, not only capital outlays. Insurance can spread the residual risk; it cannot reduce the hazard itself.

An honest account of food systems overview distinguishes three things: what has been measured, what has been modelled, and what has been assumed. Most public confusion comes from collapsing the three into one sentence (IPCC, 2021). The distinction is concrete: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Numbers carry confidence intervals, and those intervals should travel with them into any decision. In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the discipline is to write the interval next to the estimate whenever the estimate is used. A disclosed range can be planned against; an undisclosed point estimate cannot.

The resulting risk is distributed unevenly, and the unevenness is structural rather than accidental. Tenure, income, age, health and gender determine how much a household can prepare, evacuate or recover (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate numbers conceal this: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). Averages describe the size of the problem, not who carries it. Decisions taken by food systems and rural livelihoods — where to build, whom to insure, which services keep running — widen that gap or close it. A policy that protects the average household while leaving the most exposed unprotected has not solved the problem it claims to solve. For food systems and rural livelihoods, the practical question is what would have to change first.

The relevant question for food systems overview is not what will happen but what to do while the answer is still forming. That favours measures that are cheap, reversible and useful across several futures. Early warning, insurance, redundant systems and protected water sources meet that test; megaprojects tied to one scenario do not (IPCC, 2023). Pressure is building regardless: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). Within food systems and rural livelihoods, the portfolio matters more than any single measure. Robustness, not optimality, is the right criterion under uncertainty.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Spain's 2023 olive harvest halved after drought+heat, driving global olive oil prices up 112% (FAO, IOC). The event is best read as a measurement, not as a verdict. For food systems and rural livelihoods, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

The evidence is not unanimous on pace and regional detail, and the gaps should be named rather than smoothed over. Where estimates differ, the difference usually traces back to assumptions about emissions, adaptation and measurement. That does not weaken the case for action; it defines the range within which action has to be robust. As far as food systems and rural livelihoods is concerned, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 7.2 Crop Yield Impacts

Cynthia Rosenzweig (2020) puts it plainly: the potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing — food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation.

Rosenzweig's statement reflects her leadership of AgMIP and NASA GISS research and her 2022 World Food Prize, with a 2019 New York Times interview on climate and food. 'Potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing' refers to the growing likelihood that major grain regions — North America, Europe, Asia — suffer simultaneous yield shocks in the same year. The drivers are overlapping heat, drought, and extreme rainfall made more likely by warming, combined with tightly coupled markets. 'Food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation' means incremental farm-level tweaks alone are insufficient; supply chains, storage, trade, and diets must adapt together. Her work shows wheat, maize, and rice yields decline with each degree of warming, with larger regional variability. For crop yield impacts within food security and agriculture under climate change, this frames food and agriculture as systemic risk requiring diversification, resilient varieties, ecosystem-based practices, and early action on storage and distribution. For crop yield impacts, the bearing is direct: the constraint is institutional as much as physical.

Not all statements about crop yield impacts carry the same warrant. Some rest on direct measurement, some on models, some on expert judgement, and the three should not be written in the same voice (IPCC, 2021). The numbers are often firmer than the uses to which they are put: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Misreading usually happens in translation — a global average applied to a locality, a scenario treated as a forecast. In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the safeguard is to record the source, period and assumptions behind each figure used. Traceability is not bureaucracy; it is what allows a claim to be checked later.

The aggregate picture hides the distribution, and in crop yield impacts the distribution carries most of the policy content. Risk is concentrated by geography, tenure and income, and the concentration is produced by prior decisions about land, services and insurance (UNDRR, 2015). Because those decisions were made deliberately, they can be unmade deliberately. The unevenness is measurable: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). For food systems and rural livelihoods, averages are the wrong unit of account; the relevant figure is the burden carried by the group least able to carry it. A policy judged by its average effect will systematically miss the people it is meant to protect.

Any assessment of crop yield impacts will turn less on what is promised now than on what is in place when the next event arrives. Plans that exist only on paper, thresholds that trigger no action, and funds that cannot be released quickly are the recurring failure modes (UNDRR, 2015). The pressure is not hypothetical: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). Progress can be measured in lead times, in the share of exposed households covered by warning and insurance, and in how quickly services return. Within food systems and rural livelihoods, reviewing those measures on a fixed cycle works better than reviewing them after each disaster. Iteration is the difference between adaptation and repeated recovery.

The economics of crop yield impacts are usually presented as a bill, which is the least useful form in which to present them. Costs arrive early and are budgeted, while benefits arrive late and are unassigned; the asymmetry systematically biases public investment toward response and away from preparation (World Bank, 2023). Insurance and credit then transmit that bias into private decisions about where to build and what to protect. The magnitude is not marginal: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as food systems and rural livelihoods is concerned, the useful accounting separates the cost of preparing from the cost of not preparing, and prices both over the same horizon. A measure that is expensive against one budget year is often cheap against ten.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: Spain's 2023 olive harvest halved after drought+heat, driving global olive oil prices up 112% (FAO, IOC). Reported in isolation the figure looks dramatic; reported against the baseline it looks consistent. Within food systems and rural livelihoods, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

The strands point in one direction: the physical signal is established, the human consequences depend on exposure and capacity, and the response depends on decisions taken before the event. That sequence is what makes the problem tractable, and what makes delay expensive. In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 7.3 Heat and Water Stress on Crops

As Cynthia Rosenzweig (2020) argues, the potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing — food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation.

Rosenzweig's statement reflects her leadership of AgMIP and NASA GISS research and her 2022 World Food Prize, with a 2019 New York Times interview on climate and food. 'Potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing' refers to the growing likelihood that major grain regions — North America, Europe, Asia — suffer simultaneous yield shocks in the same year. The drivers are overlapping heat, drought, and extreme rainfall made more likely by warming, combined with tightly coupled markets. 'Food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation' means incremental farm-level tweaks alone are insufficient; supply chains, storage, trade, and diets must adapt together. Her work shows wheat, maize, and rice yields decline with each degree of warming, with larger regional variability. For heat and water stress on crops within food security and agriculture under climate change, this frames food and agriculture as systemic risk requiring diversification, resilient varieties, ecosystem-based practices, and early action on storage and distribution. Read against heat and water stress on crops, the point is that intention without capacity changes little.

The distribution of harm is not a detail appended to the analysis; it is the analysis. Two households can face the same event and emerge with different futures, because what they own, owe and can claim determines recovery (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate picture obscures this: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). For that reason, average-based targets can be met while the most exposed end up worse off. For food systems and rural livelihoods, the measures that matter are those judged by their effect on the bottom of the distribution, not the middle. That is also the test of whether a policy is serious.

The next decade will show whether preparation or improvisation prevails. Three signals are worth tracking: how often events exceed design thresholds, how much public spending goes to prevention rather than repair, and whether the most exposed groups gain access before a disaster rather than after it (IPCC, 2023). The baseline is already shifting: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). None of those signals is a perfect measure, but together they indicate direction. Within food systems and rural livelihoods, adaptation is not a state to be reached but a standing capacity to revise decisions as evidence changes. The test is whether the next surprise is met with a plan or with a scramble.

The case for spending early rests on a comparison that is rarely made explicit: prevention against recovery, over decades rather than one budget cycle. Damage functions and observed recovery costs point the same way (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is contested in detail but not in direction: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Prevention loses politically because its benefits are invisible: the disaster that did not happen has no constituency. As far as food systems and rural livelihoods is concerned, the fix is institutional — budget rules that reserve funds for prevention, and appraisal that counts avoided losses. Otherwise the arithmetic is sound and the incentives are not.

The strength of the claim about heat and water stress on crops varies, and that variation should be visible in the prose. Detection and attribution are well supported; regional projections carry wider bands; estimates of future damage depend on assumptions about emissions and adaptation (IPCC, 2021). A concrete example of the difference: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). A central estimate without its range is not more precise, only less honest. In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the discipline is to keep scenarios labelled as scenarios and observations labelled as observations. Where the evidence is thin, the correct response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence.

A recent marker points the same way: Spain's 2023 olive harvest halved after drought+heat, driving global olive oil prices up 112% (FAO, IOC). On its own it proves little; placed against the trend it shows the direction of travel. As far as food systems and rural livelihoods is concerned, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

Two cautions apply to everything in this section. First, most figures are aggregates, and aggregates travel badly between countries and neighbourhoods. Second, published estimates lag the climate they describe. Both cautions argue for revisiting the numbers rather than treating them as fixed. For food systems and rural livelihoods, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 7.4 Pests and Diseases

As Tim Wheeler (2013) argues, food shocks travel through prices and nutrition, striking import-dependent poor first.

Wheeler and von Braun trace how food shocks travel through prices, trade and nutrition. Import-dependent poor households absorb the first and deepest losses. Short-term food aid does not prevent the next shock. The implication for pests and diseases is a question of sequence: what has to be in place before the event, not after it.

Anyone deciding about pests and diseases is choosing under conditions that will not improve before the choice has to be made. That makes sequencing the central skill: measures that are cheap, reversible and useful under several futures go first, and irreversible commitments wait for better information (IPCC, 2022). The sequence matters because early options buy the time that later options need. The pressure is documented rather than projected: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). Within food systems and rural livelihoods, the test of a plan is not whether it is optimal for one forecast but whether it survives several. Decisions taken now will be judged by events that were not in the forecast.

Money spent before an event buys more protection than money spent after it. That claim rests on damage functions and on the repeated observation that recovery costs exceed prevention costs by a wide margin (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is not in doubt: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). What is uncertain is how benefits are distributed and how quickly they appear, which is why appraisal methods matter. As far as food systems and rural livelihoods is concerned, the relevant question is not whether prevention pays but who captures the return. Financing arrangements that ignore that question tend to fund visible damage and neglect invisible capacity.

Uncertainty in pests and diseases is not a single quantity, and treating it as one is the most common analytic error in the field. Physical uncertainty, scenario uncertainty and model uncertainty behave differently: the first narrows with data, the second depends on choices, the third on structure (IPCC, 2021). Only the first is reduced by waiting; the other two are reduced by deciding. The range is wide enough to matter for planning: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the correct response to the wide part of the range is a decision that performs acceptably across it, not a postponement until it narrows. Plans that require certainty before they begin are plans that begin too late.

The burden falls first on people with the least room to manoeuvre. Those with insecure tenure, irregular income or caring responsibilities have less capacity to move, insure or wait for compensation (IPCC, 2022). The scale is not marginal: wheat yields fall ~6% per 1°C warming (Zhao et al., 2017). Because disadvantages compound, the same event can be an inconvenience for one family and a permanent loss for another. For food systems and rural livelihoods, equity is therefore a design criterion rather than a footnote. Measures that reach the most exposed first tend to perform better for everyone else as well.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Spain's 2023 olive harvest halved after drought+heat, driving global olive oil prices up 112% (FAO, IOC). Taken alone the episode is weather; taken with the record it is a data point on a slope. In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

The argument, reduced to its shortest form, is this: the physical signal is robust, the human exposure is uneven, and the response is a choice. Each element rests on evidence of different strength, and the weakest link is not the science but the capacity to act before the event rather than after it. Within food systems and rural livelihoods, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 7.5 Case Study: Food Security and Agriculture

One documented episode makes the argument concrete: Spain's 2023 olive harvest halved after drought+heat, driving global olive oil prices up 112% (FAO, IOC). It repays attention less as an outlier than as a chain of decisions — where the hazard was allowed to concentrate, how the response was organised, and who bore the residual loss. In the work of food systems and rural livelihoods, the sequence is worth reconstructing before the next season rather than after it.

Cases of this kind are useful because they compress several mechanisms into a short period. Exposure, capacity and institutional reach become visible at once, and the gaps between them stop being abstract (UNDRR, 2015). For food systems and rural livelihoods, the episode works as a readiness test rather than a weather story.

The test of any lesson drawn from the case is whether it changes a budget line, a standard or a protocol. If it changes none of the three, it remains a story.



## 7.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Food Security and Agriculture

For food security and agriculture, the near term appears in yields and prices: by 2040 wheat yields fall 8–15% and maize 10–18% in South Asia and the Sahel, rice drops 5–10% from night heat, and tropical fish catch falls 10–20%, pushing 40–60 million more people into hunger.

By 2050, simultaneous breadbasket failures become plausible and food prices swing 20–35% in bad years; by 2100 a high-emissions path cuts staple output 20–30% without adaptation, while irrigation demand rises 30–40% (Zhao et al., 2017; FAO, 2023).

Heat-tolerant seeds, sowing 2–3 weeks earlier, drip and mulch saving 30% of water, fish ponds and village grain banks smooth the lean months.

[[TABLE:Table 7.1 Future scenarios and predictions for food security and agriculture under climate change (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | wheat −5%; maize −8% in hot zones |
| 2050 | price swings 20–35%; 40–60 m hungry |
| 2100 | staples −20–30% without adaptation |

Watch local yields per hectare, the date of the first reliable rain, and staple prices in the nearest market; crop response to heat is high-confidence, but farm-level losses depend on soil, seed choice and irrigation.

# Chapter 8: Ecosystems and Biodiversity Loss

*Theme: ecosystems and biodiversity loss*

What follows concerns ecosystems and biodiversity loss. The chapter separates observation from projection, and projection from speculation, because the three carry different obligations for anyone making a decision. Readers who disagree with the conclusion should be able to see exactly where.

## 8.1 Biodiversity and Ecosystem Services

Carl Folke (2010) puts it plainly: resilience thinking reframes crisis as reorganization, focusing on thresholds, diversity, and learning.

Folke's resilience thinking treats crises as reorganisation rather than collapse. Thresholds, diversity and learning determine whether a system absorbs a shock or flips into a less desirable state. Management therefore aims at keeping options open. For biodiversity and ecosystem services, the practical consequence is to name the decision that would have to change.

The record on biodiversity and ecosystem services is now strong enough to be treated as a trend rather than an anomaly. The record is uneven — some regions and seasons move sharply, others barely — but the aggregate direction is consistent across methods (IPCC, 2023). The headline figure is blunt: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). What remains contested is attribution of individual events and the speed of future change, not whether the baseline has shifted. That distinction shapes how conservation and land management should read the numbers: as a distribution to plan against, not a forecast to await. Where the evidence is thin, the honest response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence. Within conservation and land management, the test is whether the arrangement survives contact with a bad week.

How a problem is framed decides what counts as a solution, and biodiversity and ecosystem services is a case in point. Framed as an environmental issue, it invites a ministry and a budget line; framed as a development issue, it invites land use, housing and finance at once (IPCC, 2022). The second framing is harder to administer precisely because it cannot be assigned to a single department. The scale involved rules out a narrow frame: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). As far as conservation and land management is concerned, the practical consequence is that the frame has to be chosen before the plan, not after it. Frames are cheap to change on paper and expensive to change in an institution.

Why things behave as they do is a question with a traceable answer. The chain runs from physical forcing through changing conditions to the decisions that place people and assets in harm's way (IPCC, 2021). Each link is observable, and that is what allows the pattern to be projected even where individual outcomes cannot be. The magnitudes involved are not small: Arctic sea ice declines by ~13% per decade (NASA, 2024). In the work of conservation and land management, there is more than one point of action, and the cheapest interventions are usually the earliest. Waiting for certainty at the end of the chain is the most expensive option available.

Uncertainty in biodiversity and ecosystem services is not a single quantity, and treating it as one is the most common analytic error in the field. Physical uncertainty, scenario uncertainty and model uncertainty behave differently: the first narrows with data, the second depends on choices, the third on structure (IPCC, 2021). Only the first is reduced by waiting; the other two are reduced by deciding. The range is wide enough to matter for planning: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). For conservation and land management, the correct response to the wide part of the range is a decision that performs acceptably across it, not a postponement until it narrows. Plans that require certainty before they begin are plans that begin too late.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Coral bleaching events are becoming more frequent, threatening marine ecosystems (IPCC, NOAA). Set beside the record, the episode indicates direction without fixing the rate. Within conservation and land management, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

It is worth separating what would change the conclusion from what would merely add detail. A revision of the observed trend, or evidence that adaptation is failing faster than expected, would matter; another confirmation of the same direction would not. That test keeps attention on the claims that carry weight. Within conservation and land management, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 8.2 Habitat Loss

As Elizabeth Kolbert (2006) argues, extinction and climate change are not separate stories but one story of human planetary impact.

Kolbert's reporting treats extinction and climate change as one story of cumulative human pressure. Species loss removes the ecological margin that absorbs variability. The narrative connects field observation with paleoclimate evidence. Applied to habitat loss, the argument sets a test: does the measure still work when capacity is stretched?

It helps to ask what question habitat loss is really answering. Framed as a measurement problem, it invites better instruments; framed as a decision problem, it invites better institutions (IPCC, 2023). The difference is practical, because the second framing admits trade-offs that the first hides. The scale is already visible: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). As far as conservation and land management is concerned, the choice of framing determines which options are considered at all. A question that excludes trade-offs will produce an answer that cannot be implemented.

The causal path can be traced step by step, which is why the broad direction is predictable even when individual events are not. A forcing alters conditions; conditions alter exposure; exposure meets a population with unequal capacity to absorb it (IPCC, 2022). Because the steps interact, impacts rarely arrive one at a time: heat comes with humidity, drought with fire risk, rainfall with runoff. The same pattern is visible in the numbers: Arctic sea ice declines by ~13% per decade (NASA, 2024). In the work of conservation and land management, the practical implication is to plan for combinations rather than single hazards. Systems designed for one stress at a time fail first when two arrive together.

An honest account of habitat loss distinguishes three things: what has been measured, what has been modelled, and what has been assumed. Most public confusion comes from collapsing the three into one sentence (IPCC, 2021). The distinction is concrete: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). Numbers carry confidence intervals, and those intervals should travel with them into any decision. For conservation and land management, the discipline is to write the interval next to the estimate whenever the estimate is used. A disclosed range can be planned against; an undisclosed point estimate cannot.

Evidence on habitat loss rests on several independent lines rather than a single study. Satellites, ground stations, ocean measurements and model reanalyses now agree on direction, and disagree mainly on pace and regional detail (IPCC, 2021). One measure puts the magnitude in view: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Confidence is highest where those lines converge and lowest where one method or a short record carries the claim. Within conservation and land management, that means planning against a range rather than a point. A decision built on the central estimate alone is a decision that will be surprised.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: Coral bleaching events are becoming more frequent, threatening marine ecosystems (IPCC, NOAA). Read against the longer record, it is an illustration rather than a proof. As far as conservation and land management is concerned, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

Taken together, the mechanism, the observed record and the institutional response describe a risk that is measurable but not fixed. The practical question is not whether the trend is real, but which decisions today remain defensible across the full range of projections — and those are usually the decisions that are cheap to reverse. As far as conservation and land management is concerned, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 8.3 Species Extinction Risk

As Johan Rockström (2009) argues, we define planetary boundaries within which humanity can operate safely — six of nine boundaries are now transgressed, well outside the safe operating space.

Rockström's statement originates from the 2009 Nature paper 'A safe operating space for humanity' and its 2023 update that now finds six of nine boundaries transgressed. 'Planetary boundaries' are quantitative thresholds for climate, biosphere, freshwater, nutrients, and other Earth-system processes that define a Holocene-like stability zone. 'Operate safely' means staying within these thresholds reduces risk of abrupt, irreversible shifts. 'Six of nine transgressed' indicates humanity has left the safe zone on climate, biodiversity, land, freshwater, biogeochemical flows, and novel entities, far outside preindustrial variability. The update emphasizes interactions — transgressing one boundary tightens others, such as deforestation amplifying carbon release. The concept reframes species extinction risk from isolated sectoral problem to systemic interdependence. For ecosystems and biodiversity loss, it implies solutions must be integrated across energy, food, and ecosystem management rather than siloed. For species extinction risk, the bearing is direct: the constraint is institutional as much as physical.

Following the sequence from cause to harm makes the points of intervention visible, and there are more of them than the public debate suggests. Emissions determine the forcing; land use and insurance determine exposure; income, health and warning determine capacity; each link can be altered by policy at a different scale (UNDRR, 2015). Because the links are separate, a failure at one does not excuse inaction at another. The scale at which the chain operates is large: Arctic sea ice declines by ~13% per decade (NASA, 2024). In the work of conservation and land management, the practical value of thinking in a chain is that it shows which link is cheapest to move this year. Intervention points are political choices, not physical facts.

Not all statements about species extinction risk carry the same warrant. Some rest on direct measurement, some on models, some on expert judgement, and the three should not be written in the same voice (IPCC, 2021). The numbers are often firmer than the uses to which they are put: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). Misreading usually happens in translation — a global average applied to a locality, a scenario treated as a forecast. For conservation and land management, the safeguard is to record the source, period and assumptions behind each figure used. Traceability is not bureaucracy; it is what allows a claim to be checked later.

The evidence base behind species extinction risk is larger than its public profile suggests, and more uneven. Satellite records, station data, insurance claims and household surveys answer different questions, and they rarely agree on the same decimal place (IPCC, 2021). Where they disagree, the disagreement is usually about coverage rather than about the direction of change. Confidence is highest on aggregates and lowest on localities: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Within conservation and land management, the sensible use of the record is to establish direction and then test local plausibility rather than to import a global average wholesale. A number that cannot be traced to a source is not evidence; it is decoration.

Most disagreements about species extinction risk are not disagreements about facts. They are disagreements about weight: which risks count, over what horizon, and for whom (IPCC, 2022). That is why the same evidence can support both alarm and complacency without either side misreading the data. The scale involved is not marginal: Arctic sea ice declines by ~13% per decade (NASA, 2024). As far as conservation and land management is concerned, clarity about the weighting matters as much as clarity about the measurement. Stating the criterion in advance is what separates analysis from advocacy.

A recent marker points the same way: Coral bleaching events are becoming more frequent, threatening marine ecosystems (IPCC, NOAA). The value of such reports is that they date the change rather than explain it. In the work of conservation and land management, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

Certainty is unevenly distributed across this literature. Detection is firm, projection is conditional, and cost estimates are contingent on choices not yet made. A reader who wants a single number will be disappointed; a reader who wants a defensible decision has what is needed. In the work of conservation and land management, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 8.4 Forest Ecosystems

Carl Folke (2010) puts it plainly: resilience thinking reframes crisis as reorganization, focusing on thresholds, diversity, and learning.

Folke's resilience thinking treats crises as reorganisation rather than collapse. Thresholds, diversity and learning determine whether a system absorbs a shock or flips into a less desirable state. Management therefore aims at keeping options open. Read against forest ecosystems, the point is that intention without capacity changes little.

The strength of the claim about forest ecosystems varies, and that variation should be visible in the prose. Detection and attribution are well supported; regional projections carry wider bands; estimates of future damage depend on assumptions about emissions and adaptation (IPCC, 2021). A concrete example of the difference: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). A central estimate without its range is not more precise, only less honest. For conservation and land management, the discipline is to keep scenarios labelled as scenarios and observations labelled as observations. Where the evidence is thin, the correct response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence.

The evidence base for forest ecosystems is broad but uneven, and the unevenness is informative. Where many independent studies agree, confidence is high; where a single model family supplies the estimate, it is not (IPCC, 2021). One figure illustrates the state of knowledge: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). The appropriate response to a strong signal with weak local detail is not to wait for precision but to design for the range. Within conservation and land management, that means carrying uncertainty into the decision rather than resolving it beforehand. Plans that require certainty before acting are plans that act late.

Discussion of forest ecosystems usually begins as a technical question, and that framing hides the decisions underneath it. The technical part is real: measurement, modelling and attribution have improved sharply over two decades (IPCC, 2023). But the contested part is rarely the measurement; it is what the numbers imply for who acts, who pays and who is protected. One figure sets the scale: Arctic sea ice declines by ~13% per decade (NASA, 2024). Read that way, the question for conservation and land management is not whether the trend is real but which choices remain defensible across the plausible range. A framing that treats uncertainty as a reason to wait gets the logic backwards: uncertainty is what makes early, reversible decisions valuable. As far as conservation and land management is concerned, the practical question is what would have to change first.

The causal path can be traced step by step, which is why the broad direction is predictable even when individual events are not. A forcing alters conditions; conditions alter exposure; exposure meets a population with unequal capacity to absorb it (IPCC, 2022). Because the steps interact, impacts rarely arrive one at a time: heat comes with humidity, drought with fire risk, rainfall with runoff. The same pattern is visible in the numbers: ~800 million people face food insecurity (FAO, 2023). In the work of conservation and land management, the practical implication is to plan for combinations rather than single hazards. Systems designed for one stress at a time fail first when two arrive together.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Coral bleaching events are becoming more frequent, threatening marine ecosystems (IPCC, NOAA). It matters less as a record than as evidence that the baseline has already moved. For conservation and land management, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

What emerges is less a prediction than a test. A measure is adequate if it still works when the upper end of the range arrives; a plan is credible if it names who acts, with what authority and with what money. Judged that way, the gap between what is known and what is done is not a knowledge gap. For conservation and land management, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 8.5 Case Study: Ecosystems and Biodiversity Loss

A single episode illustrates the mechanism: Coral bleaching events are becoming more frequent, threatening marine ecosystems (IPCC, NOAA). Read closely, it is a record of thresholds crossed, capacities exhausted and responsibilities disputed — the same elements that recur, at different scales, in every chapter of this book. For conservation and land management, the episode fixes the date at which the argument stops being hypothetical.

The instructive part is the asymmetry between the event and its aftermath. Response times are measured in hours, recovery in years, and the difference between the two is usually decided before the event — by land use, insurance and local authority, all of which conservation and land management controls.

The test of any lesson drawn from the case is whether it changes a budget line, a standard or a protocol. If it changes none of the three, it remains a story.



## 8.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Ecosystems and Biodiversity Loss

For ecosystems and biodiversity, the near term is measured in bleaching and range shifts: by 2040 coral reefs face annual bleaching across 90% of tropical reefs, 15–25% of bird ranges move upslope, and mangroves lose 10–20% where seas rise fastest.

By 2050, one in ten species faces a high risk of extinction at 2 °C, and by 2100 narrow-range species face 2–3 times the extinction risk under high emissions; ecosystem services that shield coasts and pollinate crops weaken in step (IPCC, 2022; IPBES, 2019).

Marine protected networks, corridors linking forest patches, replanting diverse native species, and community ranger patrols keep ecosystems connected enough to adapt.

[[TABLE:Table 8.1 Future scenarios and predictions for ecosystems and biodiversity loss (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | bleaching hits 50–70% of reefs |
| 2050 | 1 in 10 species at high extinction risk |
| 2100 | narrow-range species risk ×2–3 |

Watch coral bleaching alerts, the arrival dates of migratory birds, and mangrove cover in your district; global reef and range trends are high-confidence, but local species losses vary with habitat fragmentation.

# Chapter 9: Health Impacts of Climate Change

*Theme: health impacts of climate change*

This chapter examines health impacts of climate change. It moves from mechanism to evidence to what can be done, and each section states its main claim before the supporting material. Where the evidence is uncertain, the text says so; where it is strong, the language is correspondingly direct.

## 9.1 Direct and Indirect Health Impacts

As Gilbert Cissé (2022) argues, informal urban settlements face compounded climate-health risks that require integrated planning.

Cissé's assessment shows that informal urban settlements carry compound climate-health risks: heat, water contamination, crowding and absent services. Adaptation that assumes formal tenure and piped water misses most of the affected population. Planning has to start from the settlement, not the grid. In the terms of direct and indirect health impacts, the claim shifts attention from prediction to preparation.

The distribution of harm is not a detail appended to the analysis; it is the analysis. Two households can face the same event and emerge with different futures, because what they own, owe and can claim determines recovery (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate picture obscures this: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). For that reason, average-based targets can be met while the most exposed end up worse off. For health systems and epidemiology, the measures that matter are those judged by their effect on the bottom of the distribution, not the middle. That is also the test of whether a policy is serious.

Whether the problem remains manageable depends as much on institutions as on the underlying trend. Early warning, land-use rules, standards and social protection reduce harm, but only where authority and funding reach the level at which risk is felt (UNDRR, 2015). The common failure is fragmentation: overlapping mandates, unshared data, responsibility falling between agencies. Coordination is a design problem with measurable features — joint protocols, common baselines, clear triggers. Within health systems and epidemiology, the test is whether its own arrangements have those features before the next event rather than after it. Where such arrangements exist, response times fall and losses follow.

The case for spending early rests on a comparison that is rarely made explicit: prevention against recovery, over decades rather than one budget cycle. Damage functions and observed recovery costs point the same way (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is contested in detail but not in direction: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Prevention loses politically because its benefits are invisible: the disaster that did not happen has no constituency. As far as health systems and epidemiology is concerned, the fix is institutional — budget rules that reserve funds for prevention, and appraisal that counts avoided losses. Otherwise the arithmetic is sound and the incentives are not.

The next decade will show whether preparation or improvisation prevails. Three signals are worth tracking: how often events exceed design thresholds, how much public spending goes to prevention rather than repair, and whether the most exposed groups gain access before a disaster rather than after it (IPCC, 2023). The baseline is already shifting: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). None of those signals is a perfect measure, but together they indicate direction. In the work of health systems and epidemiology, adaptation is not a state to be reached but a standing capacity to revise decisions as evidence changes. The test is whether the next surprise is met with a plan or with a scramble.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: European summer 2022 heatwave caused over 61,000 excess deaths, the continent's deadliest heat event (Nature Medicine, 2023). Reported in isolation the figure looks dramatic; reported against the baseline it looks consistent. As far as health systems and epidemiology is concerned, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

Two limits apply. The first is scale: global figures say little about a district, and district figures are often unavailable. The second is time: the record is long enough to detect a trend but not long enough to rule out rare extremes. Good planning treats both as design constraints rather than reasons to postpone. For health systems and epidemiology, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 9.2 Heat-Related Illness

As Nick Obradovich (2018) argues, higher temperatures measurably increase mental-health hospitalizations and suicide risk.

Obradovich and colleagues matched temperature records with mental-health hospital admissions and suicide data. Both rise with heat, and the effect persists beyond a single day. The mechanism runs through sleep, medication and social contact. For heat-related illness, the practical consequence is to name the decision that would have to change.

Institutional performance in heat-related illness depends less on formal authority than on whether mandates, money and information arrive together. Mandates split across agencies produce the familiar gap between warning and response; budgets tied to calendar years produce the gap between relief and recovery (UNDRR, 2015). Neither gap requires bad faith, only an ordinary division of labour. The strain is already visible in the numbers: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Within health systems and epidemiology, the design question is where to place the authority that can move money and people within the same week. Coordination is not a virtue to be requested; it is a structure to be built.

Money spent before an event buys more protection than money spent after it. That claim rests on damage functions and on the repeated observation that recovery costs exceed prevention costs by a wide margin (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is not in doubt: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). What is uncertain is how benefits are distributed and how quickly they appear, which is why appraisal methods matter. As far as health systems and epidemiology is concerned, the relevant question is not whether prevention pays but who captures the return. Financing arrangements that ignore that question tend to fund visible damage and neglect invisible capacity.

Anyone deciding about heat-related illness is choosing under conditions that will not improve before the choice has to be made. That makes sequencing the central skill: measures that are cheap, reversible and useful under several futures go first, and irreversible commitments wait for better information (IPCC, 2022). The sequence matters because early options buy the time that later options need. The pressure is documented rather than projected: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). In the work of health systems and epidemiology, the test of a plan is not whether it is optimal for one forecast but whether it survives several. Decisions taken now will be judged by events that were not in the forecast.

The burden falls first on people with the least room to manoeuvre. Those with insecure tenure, irregular income or caring responsibilities have less capacity to move, insure or wait for compensation (IPCC, 2022). The scale is not marginal: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Because disadvantages compound, the same event can be an inconvenience for one family and a permanent loss for another. For health systems and epidemiology, equity is therefore a design criterion rather than a footnote. Measures that reach the most exposed first tend to perform better for everyone else as well.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: Heat waves in South Asia in 2022 and 2023 caused thousands of deaths and crop losses (IPCC, 2022; WMO, 2023). On its own it proves little; placed against the trend it shows the direction of travel. In the work of health systems and epidemiology, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

None of this settles the political question of how much to spend and who should pay. It does settle a prior question: whether there is anything worth spending on. The evidence says there is, and that the returns depend on acting while the options are still open. Within health systems and epidemiology, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 9.3 Vector-Borne Diseases

Kristie Ebi (2018) puts it plainly: if you have cancer, you need the doctor to tell you how serious it is — people are suffering and dying right now; risks rise without urgent investment in health systems.

Ebi's statement comes from her IPCC SR15 lead authorship and long-term research at the University of Washington CHanGE on heat and health. 'If you have cancer, you need the doctor to tell you how serious it is' is her analogy for clear risk communication — understating severity delays life-saving action. 'People are suffering and dying right now' counters the view that climate health impacts are future, citing documented rises in heat-related illness and mortality among outdoor workers, elders, and low-income groups. 'Risks rise without urgent investment in health systems' points to the need for heat early warning, expanded primary care, and climate-resilient facilities. Ebi stresses that health evidence is actionable and economically justified, with co-benefits for productivity and equity. For vector-borne diseases within health impacts of climate change, her framing treats adaptation as public health preparedness, requiring surveillance, workforce training, and funding that reaches the front lines. Applied to vector-borne diseases, the argument sets a test: does the measure still work when capacity is stretched?

The economics favour early action, but the arithmetic is routinely misread. Damage functions show rising marginal losses with warming, while preventive spending delivers returns over decades (World Bank, 2022). The magnitudes are large: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). The obstacle is less the total than the distribution of costs and benefits: those who pay are rarely those who are protected. As far as health systems and epidemiology is concerned, the implication is to count avoided losses, not only capital outlays. Insurance can spread the residual risk; it cannot reduce the hazard itself.

The relevant question for vector-borne diseases is not what will happen but what to do while the answer is still forming. That favours measures that are cheap, reversible and useful across several futures. Early warning, insurance, redundant systems and protected water sources meet that test; megaprojects tied to one scenario do not (IPCC, 2023). Pressure is building regardless: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). In the work of health systems and epidemiology, the portfolio matters more than any single measure. Robustness, not optimality, is the right criterion under uncertainty.

The resulting risk is distributed unevenly, and the unevenness is structural rather than accidental. Tenure, income, age, health and gender determine how much a household can prepare, evacuate or recover (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate numbers conceal this: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Averages describe the size of the problem, not who carries it. Decisions taken by health systems and epidemiology — where to build, whom to insure, which services keep running — widen that gap or close it. A policy that protects the average household while leaving the most exposed unprotected has not solved the problem it claims to solve. For health systems and epidemiology, the practical question is what would have to change first.

Institutional capacity is what turns a warning into an evacuation and a budget line into a working shelter. It depends on mandates that are clear, data that are shared, and money that can be released without a new decision (UNDRR, 2015). Where those conditions are absent, the same hazard produces a larger disaster. The stakes are visible in the numbers: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Within health systems and epidemiology, capability is better tested in a drill than in a document. A plan that has never been exercised is a hypothesis.

A recent marker points the same way: European summer 2022 heatwave caused over 61,000 excess deaths, the continent's deadliest heat event (Nature Medicine, 2023). Taken alone the episode is weather; taken with the record it is a data point on a slope. For health systems and epidemiology, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

The evidence is not unanimous on pace and regional detail, and the gaps should be named rather than smoothed over. Where estimates differ, the difference usually traces back to assumptions about emissions, adaptation and measurement. That does not weaken the case for action; it defines the range within which action has to be robust. As far as health systems and epidemiology is concerned, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 9.4 Water-Borne Diseases

As Caroline Hickman (2021) argues, 45% of youth say climate feelings impair daily life, tied to perceived government inaction.

Hickman's international survey of young people found widespread distress about climate and government inaction. Respondents reported that worry interfered with daily life and decisions about the future. The sample is large but self-selected in some countries. For water-borne diseases, the bearing is direct: the constraint is institutional as much as physical.

Any assessment of water-borne diseases will turn less on what is promised now than on what is in place when the next event arrives. Plans that exist only on paper, thresholds that trigger no action, and funds that cannot be released quickly are the recurring failure modes (UNDRR, 2015). The pressure is not hypothetical: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). Progress can be measured in lead times, in the share of exposed households covered by warning and insurance, and in how quickly services return. In the work of health systems and epidemiology, reviewing those measures on a fixed cycle works better than reviewing them after each disaster. Iteration is the difference between adaptation and repeated recovery.

The aggregate picture hides the distribution, and in water-borne diseases the distribution carries most of the policy content. Risk is concentrated by geography, tenure and income, and the concentration is produced by prior decisions about land, services and insurance (UNDRR, 2015). Because those decisions were made deliberately, they can be unmade deliberately. The unevenness is measurable: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). For health systems and epidemiology, averages are the wrong unit of account; the relevant figure is the burden carried by the group least able to carry it. A policy judged by its average effect will systematically miss the people it is meant to protect.

Governance is tested at the seams between organisations. Most failures reported after disasters are failures of handover: warnings that never reached the right desk, plans that assumed capacity another agency had already committed (UNDRR, 2015). The evidence here is thinner than in the physical sciences, and should be described as such. One indication of the stakes: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). What the record does show is that authority, money and information have to move together; any one without the others stalls. Within health systems and epidemiology, the test is whether a decision can be taken where the consequence is felt, with the resources to act on it.

The economics of water-borne diseases are usually presented as a bill, which is the least useful form in which to present them. Costs arrive early and are budgeted, while benefits arrive late and are unassigned; the asymmetry systematically biases public investment toward response and away from preparation (World Bank, 2023). Insurance and credit then transmit that bias into private decisions about where to build and what to protect. The magnitude is not marginal: ~7 million air-pollution deaths occur annually (WHO, 2023). As far as health systems and epidemiology is concerned, the useful accounting separates the cost of preparing from the cost of not preparing, and prices both over the same horizon. A measure that is expensive against one budget year is often cheap against ten.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Heat waves in South Asia in 2022 and 2023 caused thousands of deaths and crop losses (IPCC, 2022; WMO, 2023). The item is useful because it fixes a date to a trend that otherwise drifts. Within health systems and epidemiology, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

The strands point in one direction: the physical signal is established, the human consequences depend on exposure and capacity, and the response depends on decisions taken before the event. That sequence is what makes the problem tractable, and what makes delay expensive. In the work of health systems and epidemiology, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 9.5 Case Study: Health Impacts of Climate Change

The case that anchors this chapter is documented and recent: European summer 2022 heatwave caused over 61,000 excess deaths, the continent's deadliest heat event (Nature Medicine, 2023). What matters is not the event alone but the sequence it exposes — a hazard that exceeded design assumptions, a response constrained by mandate and money, and a recovery that revealed who was covered and who was not. Within health systems and epidemiology, it is the clearest recent test of whether the arrangements on paper survive contact with an event.

Cases of this kind are useful because they compress several mechanisms into a short period. Exposure, capacity and institutional reach become visible at once, and the gaps between them stop being abstract (UNDRR, 2015). As far as health systems and epidemiology is concerned, the episode works as a readiness test rather than a weather story.

In the work of health systems and epidemiology, the transferable lesson is procedural: identify which decision failed first, and fix that decision rather than the symptom.



## 9.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Health Impacts of Climate Change

For health impacts, the near term shows up in clinics: by 2040 heat deaths rise 30–50% in cities above 35 °C, dengue expands 150–200 km poleward, and diarrheal cases jump 10–20% after floods.

By 2050, heat and vector-borne disease stretch health systems thin, with 500,000+ heat deaths a year already recorded; by 2100 highland malaria doubles under high emissions without control, and mental-health burden grows alongside physical illness (Watts et al., 2021; WHO, 2023).

Heat-health alerts, screened housing, oral rehydration stocks, and clinics with flood-proof power and records protect the most exposed first.

[[TABLE:Table 9.1 Future scenarios and predictions for health impacts of climate change (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | heat deaths +20–30%; dengue +100 km |
| 2050 | 500k+ heat deaths/yr; systems strained |
| 2100 | highland malaria ×2; mental burden up |

Watch heat-related admissions, dengue case counts, and clinic closures after storms; the direction of health risk is high-confidence, but local caseloads depend on sanitation, housing and health-system capacity.
