# Chapter 10: Economic Impacts and Financial Resilience

*Theme: economic impacts and financial preparedness under climate change*

The subject of this chapter is economic impacts and financial preparedness under climate change. The argument proceeds in three steps: what is happening, how we know, and what follows for decisions. Sources are cited as they are used, and contested points are marked as contested rather than presented as settled.

## 10.1 Damage Cost Estimates

As Fatih Birol (2023) argues, the energy crisis sped the transition, but fossil demand must fall far faster for 1.5°C.

Birol's World Energy Outlook tracks the gap between stated goals and investment flows. Crises have accelerated renewables while leaving fossil demand too high for 1.5°C. The series is a scenario exercise, not a forecast. The implication for damage cost estimates is a question of sequence: what has to be in place before the event, not after it.

The economics of damage cost estimates are usually presented as a bill, which is the least useful form in which to present them. Costs arrive early and are budgeted, while benefits arrive late and are unassigned; the asymmetry systematically biases public investment toward response and away from preparation (World Bank, 2023). Insurance and credit then transmit that bias into private decisions about where to build and what to protect. The magnitude is not marginal: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). In the work of finance ministries and insurers, the useful accounting separates the cost of preparing from the cost of not preparing, and prices both over the same horizon. A measure that is expensive against one budget year is often cheap against ten.

Most disagreements about damage cost estimates are not disagreements about facts. They are disagreements about weight: which risks count, over what horizon, and for whom (IPCC, 2022). That is why the same evidence can support both alarm and complacency without either side misreading the data. The scale involved is not marginal: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). For finance ministries and insurers, clarity about the weighting matters as much as clarity about the measurement. Stating the criterion in advance is what separates analysis from advocacy.

The evidence base behind damage cost estimates is larger than its public profile suggests, and more uneven. Satellite records, station data, insurance claims and household surveys answer different questions, and they rarely agree on the same decimal place (IPCC, 2021). Where they disagree, the disagreement is usually about coverage rather than about the direction of change. Confidence is highest on aggregates and lowest on localities: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). Within finance ministries and insurers, the sensible use of the record is to establish direction and then test local plausibility rather than to import a global average wholesale. A number that cannot be traced to a source is not evidence; it is decoration.

Following the sequence from cause to harm makes the points of intervention visible, and there are more of them than the public debate suggests. Emissions determine the forcing; land use and insurance determine exposure; income, health and warning determine capacity; each link can be altered by policy at a different scale (UNDRR, 2015). Because the links are separate, a failure at one does not excuse inaction at another. The scale at which the chain operates is large: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as finance ministries and insurers is concerned, the practical value of thinking in a chain is that it shows which link is cheapest to move this year. Intervention points are political choices, not physical facts.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Hurricane Ian (Sept 2022) caused $113 billion in US damages and was the costliest Florida hurricane (NOAA NHC). Read against the longer record, it is an illustration rather than a proof. In the work of finance ministries and insurers, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

Readers should know where this section stands on the evidence ladder. The direction of change is established; the pace is estimated; the local consequences are modelled. Statements that blur those levels are the ones most likely to mislead, and they are also the easiest to check. In the work of finance ministries and insurers, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 10.2 Sectoral Impacts

Andrew Dessler (2021) puts it plainly: greenhouse physics has been settled since the 1800s; debate is about impacts and choices, not reality.

Dessler points out that the physics of the greenhouse effect has been settled since the nineteenth century. What remains open is the magnitude of impacts and the choices that determine them. Public debate often confuses the two. In the terms of sectoral impacts, the claim shifts attention from prediction to preparation.

Discussion of sectoral impacts usually begins as a technical question, and that framing hides the decisions underneath it. The technical part is real: measurement, modelling and attribution have improved sharply over two decades (IPCC, 2023). But the contested part is rarely the measurement; it is what the numbers imply for who acts, who pays and who is protected. One figure sets the scale: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Read that way, the question for finance ministries and insurers is not whether the trend is real but which choices remain defensible across the plausible range. A framing that treats uncertainty as a reason to wait gets the logic backwards: uncertainty is what makes early, reversible decisions valuable. For finance ministries and insurers, that is the difference between a plan that can be defended and one that merely exists.

The evidence base for sectoral impacts is broad but uneven, and the unevenness is informative. Where many independent studies agree, confidence is high; where a single model family supplies the estimate, it is not (IPCC, 2021). One figure illustrates the state of knowledge: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). The appropriate response to a strong signal with weak local detail is not to wait for precision but to design for the range. Within finance ministries and insurers, that means carrying uncertainty into the decision rather than resolving it beforehand. Plans that require certainty before acting are plans that act late.

Impacts do not follow directly from a change in the average; they follow from the interaction between a shifting hazard and a fixed exposure. A house built to a hundred-year flood standard meets a twenty-five-year event with alarming regularity once the distribution has moved (IPCC, 2022). Nothing about the house changed; the standard it was designed against did. The shift is quantifiable: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as finance ministries and insurers is concerned, the lesson is that design standards have a shelf life and need a review date attached to them. An unrevised standard is a decision to accept a higher level of risk than the one originally debated.

The case for spending early rests on a comparison that is rarely made explicit: prevention against recovery, over decades rather than one budget cycle. Damage functions and observed recovery costs point the same way (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is contested in detail but not in direction: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Prevention loses politically because its benefits are invisible: the disaster that did not happen has no constituency. In the work of finance ministries and insurers, the fix is institutional — budget rules that reserve funds for prevention, and appraisal that counts avoided losses. Otherwise the arithmetic is sound and the incentives are not.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: US 2023 billion-dollar disasters hit record 28 events, costing $92.9B (NOAA NCEI). The value of such reports is that they date the change rather than explain it. For finance ministries and insurers, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

What the section establishes is narrower than a prediction and firmer than an opinion. It establishes that the direction is known, that the costs of inaction are concentrated on those least able to bear them, and that the available measures are known well enough to be tested. For finance ministries and insurers, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 10.3 Labor Productivity Loss

As Naomi Klein (2014) argues, climate change changes everything about how we live, consume, and govern.

Klein connects climate policy to the economic model that produced it. She argues that market instruments alone cannot deliver the pace required. The claim is a political argument rather than an empirical finding. For labor productivity loss, the practical consequence is to name the decision that would have to change.

The record on labor productivity loss is now strong enough to be treated as a trend rather than an anomaly. The record is uneven — some regions and seasons move sharply, others barely — but the aggregate direction is consistent across methods (IPCC, 2023). The headline figure is blunt: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). What remains contested is attribution of individual events and the speed of future change, not whether the baseline has shifted. That distinction shapes how finance ministries and insurers should read the numbers: as a distribution to plan against, not a forecast to await. Where the evidence is thin, the honest response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence. Within finance ministries and insurers, the test is whether the arrangement survives contact with a bad week.

Between the physical change and the harm there is a sequence, and each step can be examined on its own. A shift in the mean moves the tail of the distribution first; the tail meets exposure; exposure meets capacity; capacity fails at thresholds rather than gradually (IPCC, 2022). That is why impacts arrive in jumps and why the same event produces different outcomes in neighbouring districts. The arithmetic of the shift is blunt: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as finance ministries and insurers is concerned, the useful question is which threshold comes first and what happens on the day it is crossed. Thresholds are where a gradual problem becomes a sudden one.

Money spent before an event buys more protection than money spent after it. That claim rests on damage functions and on the repeated observation that recovery costs exceed prevention costs by a wide margin (World Bank, 2022). The order of magnitude is not in doubt: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). What is uncertain is how benefits are distributed and how quickly they appear, which is why appraisal methods matter. In the work of finance ministries and insurers, the relevant question is not whether prevention pays but who captures the return. Financing arrangements that ignore that question tend to fund visible damage and neglect invisible capacity.

How a problem is framed decides what counts as a solution, and labor productivity loss is a case in point. Framed as an environmental issue, it invites a ministry and a budget line; framed as a development issue, it invites land use, housing and finance at once (IPCC, 2022). The second framing is harder to administer precisely because it cannot be assigned to a single department. The scale involved rules out a narrow frame: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). For finance ministries and insurers, the practical consequence is that the frame has to be chosen before the plan, not after it. Frames are cheap to change on paper and expensive to change in an institution.

A recent marker points the same way: Hurricane Ian (Sept 2022) caused $113 billion in US damages and was the costliest Florida hurricane (NOAA NHC). It matters less as a record than as evidence that the baseline has already moved. Within finance ministries and insurers, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

It is worth separating what would change the conclusion from what would merely add detail. A revision of the observed trend, or evidence that adaptation is failing faster than expected, would matter; another confirmation of the same direction would not. That test keeps attention on the claims that carry weight. Within finance ministries and insurers, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 10.4 Infrastructure Damage

As Marshall Burke (2015) argues, economic output peaks at cool temperatures and declines sharply with heat, with non-linear losses for warm countries.

Burke and colleagues found that economic output peaks at moderate temperatures and falls sharply in hot conditions. Losses are non-linear and concentrated in tropical and already-warm economies. The result implies that warming redistributes income as much as it destroys it. Applied to infrastructure damage, the argument sets a test: does the measure still work when capacity is stretched?

The chain of causation is short enough to follow and long enough to intervene in. Physical change alters the frequency and intensity of events; exposure determines who meets them; capacity determines what happens next (IPCC, 2022). Because the links are separable, they can be addressed separately — and the cheapest link is usually exposure. The pattern is not hypothetical: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as finance ministries and insurers is concerned, the lesson is to intervene where the leverage is greatest rather than where the damage is most visible. Response is necessary; prevention is cheaper.

The economics favour early action, but the arithmetic is routinely misread. Damage functions show rising marginal losses with warming, while preventive spending delivers returns over decades (World Bank, 2022). The magnitudes are large: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). The obstacle is less the total than the distribution of costs and benefits: those who pay are rarely those who are protected. In the work of finance ministries and insurers, the implication is to count avoided losses, not only capital outlays. Insurance can spread the residual risk; it cannot reduce the hazard itself.

It helps to ask what question infrastructure damage is really answering. Framed as a measurement problem, it invites better instruments; framed as a decision problem, it invites better institutions (IPCC, 2023). The difference is practical, because the second framing admits trade-offs that the first hides. The scale is already visible: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). For finance ministries and insurers, the choice of framing determines which options are considered at all. A question that excludes trade-offs will produce an answer that cannot be implemented.

Evidence on infrastructure damage rests on several independent lines rather than a single study. Satellites, ground stations, ocean measurements and model reanalyses now agree on direction, and disagree mainly on pace and regional detail (IPCC, 2021). One measure puts the magnitude in view: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Confidence is highest where those lines converge and lowest where one method or a short record carries the claim. Within finance ministries and insurers, that means planning against a range rather than a point. A decision built on the central estimate alone is a decision that will be surprised.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: US 2023 billion-dollar disasters hit record 28 events, costing $92.9B (NOAA NCEI). Treated as a single observation it invites over-reading; treated as one of many it is informative. As far as finance ministries and insurers is concerned, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

Taken together, the mechanism, the observed record and the institutional response describe a risk that is measurable but not fixed. The practical question is not whether the trend is real, but which decisions today remain defensible across the full range of projections — and those are usually the decisions that are cheap to reverse. As far as finance ministries and insurers is concerned, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 10.5 Case Study: Economic Impacts and Financial Resilience

One documented episode makes the argument concrete: Hurricane Ian (Sept 2022) caused $113 billion in US damages and was the costliest Florida hurricane (NOAA NHC). It repays attention less as an outlier than as a chain of decisions — where the hazard was allowed to concentrate, how the response was organised, and who bore the residual loss. As far as finance ministries and insurers is concerned, the record supplies both the warning and the timetable.

Cases of this kind are useful because they compress several mechanisms into a short period. Exposure, capacity and institutional reach become visible at once, and the gaps between them stop being abstract (UNDRR, 2015). In the work of finance ministries and insurers, the episode works as a readiness test rather than a weather story.

The test of any lesson drawn from the case is whether it changes a budget line, a standard or a protocol. If it changes none of the three, it remains a story.



## 10.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Economic Impacts and Financial Resilience

For economic impacts and financial resilience, the near term is visible in budgets: by 2040 climate disasters cost 2–4% of GDP a year in exposed provinces, coastal insurance premiums rise 30–60%, and heat cuts work hours 10–15%.

By 2050, loss and damage claims and adaptation costs together reach hundreds of billions a year, and by 2100 a high-emissions path shaves 15–25% off incomes in vulnerable deltas compared with a low-emissions path (IPCC, 2022; World Bank, 2022).

Emergency funds equal to three months of expenses, micro-insurance pools, diversified incomes, and building codes for wind, flood and heat reduce the shock.

[[TABLE:Table 10.1 Future scenarios and predictions for economic impacts and financial preparedness under climate change (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | disaster cost 1–2% GDP; premiums +20% |
| 2050 | hundreds of billions/yr; adaptation gap |
| 2100 | incomes −15–25% in vulnerable deltas |

Watch local insurance renewals, disaster-related budget reallocations, and days of work lost to heat; global cost estimates are medium-confidence, but household exposure can be tracked directly.

# Chapter 11: Migration and Displacement

*Theme: climate migration and displacement*

This chapter deals with climate migration and displacement. Each section opens with a claim, tests it against the evidence and draws out the implication for the people who have to act on it. The aim is not completeness but a defensible line through a large literature.

## 11.1 Drivers of Climate Migration

Diana Liverman (2020) puts it plainly: climate justice means accounting for who is most responsible, who is most vulnerable, and that those least responsible for emissions bear the greatest burdens.

Liverman's climate justice scholarship, including her 2019 testimony to the Arizona House and IPCC authorship, underpins her statement on responsibility and vulnerability. 'Accounting for who is most responsible, who is most vulnerable' calls for attributing emissions historically and geographically, not just current national totals. 'Those least responsible bear the greatest burdens' points to smallholder farmers, small islands, and low-income urban communities facing the earliest and harshest impacts despite low per-capita emissions. Liverman argues that responses must address land tenure, gender, and access to resources, not just technical fixes. She emphasizes that top-down prescriptions often fail when they ignore local knowledge and power imbalances. For drivers of climate migration within climate migration and displacement, her lens requires assessing interventions by who benefits, who pays, and who decides, integrating equity metrics into planning and evaluation. Read against drivers of climate migration, the point is that intention without capacity changes little.

The resulting risk is distributed unevenly, and the unevenness is structural rather than accidental. Tenure, income, age, health and gender determine how much a household can prepare, evacuate or recover (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate numbers conceal this: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Averages describe the size of the problem, not who carries it. Decisions taken by migration policy and urban services — where to build, whom to insure, which services keep running — widen that gap or close it. A policy that protects the average household while leaving the most exposed unprotected has not solved the problem it claims to solve. As far as migration policy and urban services is concerned, that is the difference between a plan that can be defended and one that merely exists.

An honest account of drivers of climate migration distinguishes three things: what has been measured, what has been modelled, and what has been assumed. Most public confusion comes from collapsing the three into one sentence (IPCC, 2021). The distinction is concrete: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Numbers carry confidence intervals, and those intervals should travel with them into any decision. In the work of migration policy and urban services, the discipline is to write the interval next to the estimate whenever the estimate is used. A disclosed range can be planned against; an undisclosed point estimate cannot.

Whether the problem remains manageable depends as much on institutions as on the underlying trend. Early warning, land-use rules, standards and social protection reduce harm, but only where authority and funding reach the level at which risk is felt (UNDRR, 2015). The common failure is fragmentation: overlapping mandates, unshared data, responsibility falling between agencies. Coordination is a design problem with measurable features — joint protocols, common baselines, clear triggers. For migration policy and urban services, the test is whether its own arrangements have those features before the next event rather than after it. Where such arrangements exist, response times fall and losses follow.

The relevant question for drivers of climate migration is not what will happen but what to do while the answer is still forming. That favours measures that are cheap, reversible and useful across several futures. Early warning, insurance, redundant systems and protected water sources meet that test; megaprojects tied to one scenario do not (IPCC, 2023). Pressure is building regardless: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Within migration policy and urban services, the portfolio matters more than any single measure. Robustness, not optimality, is the right criterion under uncertainty.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Typhoon Doksuri flooded Beijing in July 2023 with heaviest rain in 140 years, 1 million displaced (China CMA, Reuters). On its own it proves little; placed against the trend it shows the direction of travel. For migration policy and urban services, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

Two cautions apply to everything in this section. First, most figures are aggregates, and aggregates travel badly between countries and neighbourhoods. Second, published estimates lag the climate they describe. Both cautions argue for revisiting the numbers rather than treating them as fixed. As far as migration policy and urban services is concerned, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 11.2 Internal Displacement

As Saleemul Huq (2020) argues, it is adapt or die — adaptation is survival, and loss-and-damage must be permanent in the Paris Agreement for vulnerable nations.

Huq's statement was delivered repeatedly at COPs from 2015 to 2023 and reflects his leadership at ICCCAD in Dhaka and IPCC authorship from AR3 to AR5. 'Adapt or die' was his stark shorthand that for low-lying Bangladesh and small islands, adaptation is not optional but survival. 'Loss-and-damage must be permanent in the Paris Agreement' refers to his advocacy for a third pillar beyond mitigation and adaptation to address unavoidable harms. The phrase captures that vulnerable nations face impacts they did not cause, raising justice and finance obligations. Huq linked local experience — cyclones, floods, salinity — to global negotiations, insisting that affected communities have voice. His work bridges research on community-based adaptation with policy for climate finance and compensation. For internal displacement within climate migration and displacement, his perspective centers equity, local leadership, and the limits of adaptation when warming exceeds coping capacity. The implication for internal displacement is a question of sequence: what has to be in place before the event, not after it.

Not all statements about internal displacement carry the same warrant. Some rest on direct measurement, some on models, some on expert judgement, and the three should not be written in the same voice (IPCC, 2021). The numbers are often firmer than the uses to which they are put: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Misreading usually happens in translation — a global average applied to a locality, a scenario treated as a forecast. In the work of migration policy and urban services, the safeguard is to record the source, period and assumptions behind each figure used. Traceability is not bureaucracy; it is what allows a claim to be checked later.

Institutional performance in internal displacement depends less on formal authority than on whether mandates, money and information arrive together. Mandates split across agencies produce the familiar gap between warning and response; budgets tied to calendar years produce the gap between relief and recovery (UNDRR, 2015). Neither gap requires bad faith, only an ordinary division of labour. The strain is already visible in the numbers: ~20 million people are displaced by climate annually (UNHCR, 2023). For migration policy and urban services, the design question is where to place the authority that can move money and people within the same week. Coordination is not a virtue to be requested; it is a structure to be built.

Any assessment of internal displacement will turn less on what is promised now than on what is in place when the next event arrives. Plans that exist only on paper, thresholds that trigger no action, and funds that cannot be released quickly are the recurring failure modes (UNDRR, 2015). The pressure is not hypothetical: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Progress can be measured in lead times, in the share of exposed households covered by warning and insurance, and in how quickly services return. Within migration policy and urban services, reviewing those measures on a fixed cycle works better than reviewing them after each disaster. Iteration is the difference between adaptation and repeated recovery.

The aggregate picture hides the distribution, and in internal displacement the distribution carries most of the policy content. Risk is concentrated by geography, tenure and income, and the concentration is produced by prior decisions about land, services and insurance (UNDRR, 2015). Because those decisions were made deliberately, they can be unmade deliberately. The unevenness is measurable: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). As far as migration policy and urban services is concerned, averages are the wrong unit of account; the relevant figure is the burden carried by the group least able to carry it. A policy judged by its average effect will systematically miss the people it is meant to protect.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: Mediterranean wildfires in Greece (July 2023) forced 20,000 evacuations on Rhodes, largest in Greek history (Greek Civil Protection). Taken alone the episode is weather; taken with the record it is a data point on a slope. Within migration policy and urban services, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

The argument, reduced to its shortest form, is this: the physical signal is robust, the human exposure is uneven, and the response is a choice. Each element rests on evidence of different strength, and the weakest link is not the science but the capacity to act before the event rather than after it. In the work of migration policy and urban services, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 11.3 Cross-Border Movement

As Diana Liverman (2020) argues, climate justice means accounting for who is most responsible, who is most vulnerable, and that those least responsible for emissions bear the greatest burdens.

Liverman's climate justice scholarship, including her 2019 testimony to the Arizona House and IPCC authorship, underpins her statement on responsibility and vulnerability. 'Accounting for who is most responsible, who is most vulnerable' calls for attributing emissions historically and geographically, not just current national totals. 'Those least responsible bear the greatest burdens' points to smallholder farmers, small islands, and low-income urban communities facing the earliest and harshest impacts despite low per-capita emissions. Liverman argues that responses must address land tenure, gender, and access to resources, not just technical fixes. She emphasizes that top-down prescriptions often fail when they ignore local knowledge and power imbalances. For cross-border movement within climate migration and displacement, her lens requires assessing interventions by who benefits, who pays, and who decides, integrating equity metrics into planning and evaluation. In the terms of cross-border movement, the claim shifts attention from prediction to preparation.

Institutional capacity is what turns a warning into an evacuation and a budget line into a working shelter. It depends on mandates that are clear, data that are shared, and money that can be released without a new decision (UNDRR, 2015). Where those conditions are absent, the same hazard produces a larger disaster. The stakes are visible in the numbers: ~20 million people are displaced by climate annually (UNHCR, 2023). For migration policy and urban services, capability is better tested in a drill than in a document. A plan that has never been exercised is a hypothesis.

The next decade will show whether preparation or improvisation prevails. Three signals are worth tracking: how often events exceed design thresholds, how much public spending goes to prevention rather than repair, and whether the most exposed groups gain access before a disaster rather than after it (IPCC, 2023). The baseline is already shifting: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). None of those signals is a perfect measure, but together they indicate direction. Within migration policy and urban services, adaptation is not a state to be reached but a standing capacity to revise decisions as evidence changes. The test is whether the next surprise is met with a plan or with a scramble.

The distribution of harm is not a detail appended to the analysis; it is the analysis. Two households can face the same event and emerge with different futures, because what they own, owe and can claim determines recovery (IPCC, 2022). The aggregate picture obscures this: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). For that reason, average-based targets can be met while the most exposed end up worse off. As far as migration policy and urban services is concerned, the measures that matter are those judged by their effect on the bottom of the distribution, not the middle. That is also the test of whether a policy is serious.

The strength of the claim about cross-border movement varies, and that variation should be visible in the prose. Detection and attribution are well supported; regional projections carry wider bands; estimates of future damage depend on assumptions about emissions and adaptation (IPCC, 2021). A concrete example of the difference: ~20 million people are displaced by climate annually (UNHCR, 2023). A central estimate without its range is not more precise, only less honest. In the work of migration policy and urban services, the discipline is to keep scenarios labelled as scenarios and observations labelled as observations. Where the evidence is thin, the correct response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence.

A recent marker points the same way: Amazon drought in late 2023 lowered Rio Negro to 120-year low, isolating 500,000 people (Brazil INPE, Reuters). The item is useful because it fixes a date to a trend that otherwise drifts. As far as migration policy and urban services is concerned, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

Two limits apply. The first is scale: global figures say little about a district, and district figures are often unavailable. The second is time: the record is long enough to detect a trend but not long enough to rule out rare extremes. Good planning treats both as design constraints rather than reasons to postpone. For migration policy and urban services, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 11.4 Legal Status and Rights

Saleemul Huq (2020) puts it plainly: it is adapt or die — adaptation is survival, and loss-and-damage must be permanent in the Paris Agreement for vulnerable nations.

Huq's statement was delivered repeatedly at COPs from 2015 to 2023 and reflects his leadership at ICCCAD in Dhaka and IPCC authorship from AR3 to AR5. 'Adapt or die' was his stark shorthand that for low-lying Bangladesh and small islands, adaptation is not optional but survival. 'Loss-and-damage must be permanent in the Paris Agreement' refers to his advocacy for a third pillar beyond mitigation and adaptation to address unavoidable harms. The phrase captures that vulnerable nations face impacts they did not cause, raising justice and finance obligations. Huq linked local experience — cyclones, floods, salinity — to global negotiations, insisting that affected communities have voice. His work bridges research on community-based adaptation with policy for climate finance and compensation. For legal status and rights within climate migration and displacement, his perspective centers equity, local leadership, and the limits of adaptation when warming exceeds coping capacity. For legal status and rights, the practical consequence is to name the decision that would have to change.

Anyone deciding about legal status and rights is choosing under conditions that will not improve before the choice has to be made. That makes sequencing the central skill: measures that are cheap, reversible and useful under several futures go first, and irreversible commitments wait for better information (IPCC, 2022). The sequence matters because early options buy the time that later options need. The pressure is documented rather than projected: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Within migration policy and urban services, the test of a plan is not whether it is optimal for one forecast but whether it survives several. Decisions taken now will be judged by events that were not in the forecast.

The burden falls first on people with the least room to manoeuvre. Those with insecure tenure, irregular income or caring responsibilities have less capacity to move, insure or wait for compensation (IPCC, 2022). The scale is not marginal: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021). Because disadvantages compound, the same event can be an inconvenience for one family and a permanent loss for another. As far as migration policy and urban services is concerned, equity is therefore a design criterion rather than a footnote. Measures that reach the most exposed first tend to perform better for everyone else as well.

Uncertainty in legal status and rights is not a single quantity, and treating it as one is the most common analytic error in the field. Physical uncertainty, scenario uncertainty and model uncertainty behave differently: the first narrows with data, the second depends on choices, the third on structure (IPCC, 2021). Only the first is reduced by waiting; the other two are reduced by deciding. The range is wide enough to matter for planning: ~20 million people are displaced by climate annually (UNHCR, 2023). In the work of migration policy and urban services, the correct response to the wide part of the range is a decision that performs acceptably across it, not a postponement until it narrows. Plans that require certainty before they begin are plans that begin too late.

Governance is tested at the seams between organisations. Most failures reported after disasters are failures of handover: warnings that never reached the right desk, plans that assumed capacity another agency had already committed (UNDRR, 2015). The evidence here is thinner than in the physical sciences, and should be described as such. One indication of the stakes: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). What the record does show is that authority, money and information have to move together; any one without the others stalls. For migration policy and urban services, the test is whether a decision can be taken where the consequence is felt, with the resources to act on it.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Libya's Derna dam collapse after Storm Daniel (Sept 2023) killed >4,300 and displaced 40,000 (UN OCHA, Libyan Red Crescent). What the report adds is timing, not a new mechanism. In the work of migration policy and urban services, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

None of this settles the political question of how much to spend and who should pay. It does settle a prior question: whether there is anything worth spending on. The evidence says there is, and that the returns depend on acting while the options are still open. Within migration policy and urban services, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 11.5 Case Study: Migration and Displacement

A single episode illustrates the mechanism: Typhoon Doksuri flooded Beijing in July 2023 with heaviest rain in 140 years, 1 million displaced (China CMA, Reuters). Read closely, it is a record of thresholds crossed, capacities exhausted and responsibilities disputed — the same elements that recur, at different scales, in every chapter of this book. In the work of migration policy and urban services, the sequence is worth reconstructing before the next season rather than after it.

Cases of this kind are useful because they compress several mechanisms into a short period. Exposure, capacity and institutional reach become visible at once, and the gaps between them stop being abstract (UNDRR, 2015). For migration policy and urban services, the episode works as a readiness test rather than a weather story.

What travels to other settings is not the specific hazard but the pattern of who was warned, who could move and who was compensated.



## 11.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Migration and Displacement

For migration and displacement, the near term is counted in people on the move: by 2040, 15–25 million a year are displaced by floods, storms and drought, and cities such as Dhaka, Lagos and Manila absorb 300,000–500,000 climate newcomers annually.

By 2050, slow-onset loss of farmland and coastal land adds to sudden displacement, and by 2100 a high-emissions path displaces 100–150 million cumulatively from sub-3 m coasts and drying farmland (UNHCR, 2023; IPCC, 2022).

Legal aid, portable IDs and health records, rental support, and receiving-city plans for water, jobs and schools turn displacement from crisis into managed transition.

[[TABLE:Table 11.1 Future scenarios and predictions for climate migration and displacement (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | 15–20 m displaced/yr; +300k city arrivals |
| 2050 | slow-onset adds to sudden moves |
| 2100 | 100–150 m cumulative (high path) |

Watch arrival numbers in your city, shelter occupancy, and school enrollment of newcomer children; displacement totals are medium-confidence, but local inflows are recorded and can guide planning.

# Chapter 12: Conflict, Security and Stability

*Theme: climate change, conflict and security*

What follows concerns climate change, conflict and security. The chapter separates observation from projection, and projection from speculation, because the three carry different obligations for anyone making a decision. Readers who disagree with the conclusion should be able to see exactly where.

## 12.1 Resource Competition

As Solomon Hsiang (2014) argues, climate variability is robustly linked to conflict risk, though institutions decide whether stress becomes violence.

Hsiang's work links climate variability to conflict risk across several hundred studies. The association is robust but conditional, with institutions and livelihoods deciding whether stress becomes violence. Climate is a risk multiplier, not a cause. For resource competition, the bearing is direct: the constraint is institutional as much as physical.

Governance is tested at the seams between organisations. Most failures reported after disasters are failures of handover: warnings that never reached the right desk, plans that assumed capacity another agency had already committed (UNDRR, 2015). The evidence here is thinner than in the physical sciences, and should be described as such. One indication of the stakes: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). What the record does show is that authority, money and information have to move together; any one without the others stalls. Within security services and local government, the test is whether a decision can be taken where the consequence is felt, with the resources to act on it.

How a problem is framed decides what counts as a solution, and resource competition is a case in point. Framed as an environmental issue, it invites a ministry and a budget line; framed as a development issue, it invites land use, housing and finance at once (IPCC, 2022). The second framing is harder to administer precisely because it cannot be assigned to a single department. The scale involved rules out a narrow frame: ~20 million people are displaced by climate annually (UNHCR, 2023). As far as security services and local government is concerned, the practical consequence is that the frame has to be chosen before the plan, not after it. Frames are cheap to change on paper and expensive to change in an institution.

The record on resource competition is now strong enough to be treated as a trend rather than an anomaly. The record is uneven — some regions and seasons move sharply, others barely — but the aggregate direction is consistent across methods (IPCC, 2023). The headline figure is blunt: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). What remains contested is attribution of individual events and the speed of future change, not whether the baseline has shifted. That distinction shapes how security services and local government should read the numbers: as a distribution to plan against, not a forecast to await. Where the evidence is thin, the honest response is a stated limitation rather than a confident sentence. In the work of security services and local government, the implication is a shorter list of decisions than the analysis suggests.

Why things behave as they do is a question with a traceable answer. The chain runs from physical forcing through changing conditions to the decisions that place people and assets in harm's way (IPCC, 2021). Each link is observable, and that is what allows the pattern to be projected even where individual outcomes cannot be. The magnitudes involved are not small: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). For security services and local government, there is more than one point of action, and the cheapest interventions are usually the earliest. Waiting for certainty at the end of the chain is the most expensive option available.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Horn of Africa's 2022 failed rainy seasons pushed 22 million into acute food insecurity (FEWS NET, IPC). The value of such reports is that they date the change rather than explain it. Within security services and local government, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

Certainty is unevenly distributed across this literature. Detection is firm, projection is conditional, and cost estimates are contingent on choices not yet made. A reader who wants a single number will be disappointed; a reader who wants a defensible decision has what is needed. Within security services and local government, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 12.2 Climate and Violence Evidence

As Solomon Hsiang (2014) argues, climate variability is robustly linked to conflict risk, though institutions decide whether stress becomes violence.

Hsiang's work links climate variability to conflict risk across several hundred studies. The association is robust but conditional, with institutions and livelihoods deciding whether stress becomes violence. Climate is a risk multiplier, not a cause. Read against climate and violence evidence, the point is that intention without capacity changes little.

It helps to ask what question climate and violence evidence is really answering. Framed as a measurement problem, it invites better instruments; framed as a decision problem, it invites better institutions (IPCC, 2023). The difference is practical, because the second framing admits trade-offs that the first hides. The scale is already visible: ~20 million people are displaced by climate annually (UNHCR, 2023). As far as security services and local government is concerned, the choice of framing determines which options are considered at all. A question that excludes trade-offs will produce an answer that cannot be implemented.

Evidence on climate and violence evidence rests on several independent lines rather than a single study. Satellites, ground stations, ocean measurements and model reanalyses now agree on direction, and disagree mainly on pace and regional detail (IPCC, 2021). One measure puts the magnitude in view: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Confidence is highest where those lines converge and lowest where one method or a short record carries the claim. In the work of security services and local government, that means planning against a range rather than a point. A decision built on the central estimate alone is a decision that will be surprised.

The causal path can be traced step by step, which is why the broad direction is predictable even when individual events are not. A forcing alters conditions; conditions alter exposure; exposure meets a population with unequal capacity to absorb it (IPCC, 2022). Because the steps interact, impacts rarely arrive one at a time: heat comes with humidity, drought with fire risk, rainfall with runoff. The same pattern is visible in the numbers: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). For security services and local government, the practical implication is to plan for combinations rather than single hazards. Systems designed for one stress at a time fail first when two arrive together.

Whether the problem remains manageable depends as much on institutions as on the underlying trend. Early warning, land-use rules, standards and social protection reduce harm, but only where authority and funding reach the level at which risk is felt (UNDRR, 2015). The common failure is fragmentation: overlapping mandates, unshared data, responsibility falling between agencies. Coordination is a design problem with measurable features — joint protocols, common baselines, clear triggers. Within security services and local government, the test is whether its own arrangements have those features before the next event rather than after it. Where such arrangements exist, response times fall and losses follow.

Current reporting adds detail rather than a new direction: Brazil's Rio Grande do Sul floods (Apr-May 2024) displaced 600,000, deadliest climate disaster in state history (Brazil Civil Defense). It matters less as a record than as evidence that the baseline has already moved. As far as security services and local government is concerned, the item is useful mainly as a date stamp on a trend.

What emerges is less a prediction than a test. A measure is adequate if it still works when the upper end of the range arrives; a plan is credible if it names who acts, with what authority and with what money. Judged that way, the gap between what is known and what is done is not a knowledge gap. As far as security services and local government is concerned, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 12.3 Fragile States

Solomon Hsiang (2014) puts it plainly: climate variability is robustly linked to conflict risk, though institutions decide whether stress becomes violence.

Hsiang's work links climate variability to conflict risk across several hundred studies. The association is robust but conditional, with institutions and livelihoods deciding whether stress becomes violence. Climate is a risk multiplier, not a cause. The implication for fragile states is a question of sequence: what has to be in place before the event, not after it.

The evidence base behind fragile states is larger than its public profile suggests, and more uneven. Satellite records, station data, insurance claims and household surveys answer different questions, and they rarely agree on the same decimal place (IPCC, 2021). Where they disagree, the disagreement is usually about coverage rather than about the direction of change. Confidence is highest on aggregates and lowest on localities: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). In the work of security services and local government, the sensible use of the record is to establish direction and then test local plausibility rather than to import a global average wholesale. A number that cannot be traced to a source is not evidence; it is decoration.

Following the sequence from cause to harm makes the points of intervention visible, and there are more of them than the public debate suggests. Emissions determine the forcing; land use and insurance determine exposure; income, health and warning determine capacity; each link can be altered by policy at a different scale (UNDRR, 2015). Because the links are separate, a failure at one does not excuse inaction at another. The scale at which the chain operates is large: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). For security services and local government, the practical value of thinking in a chain is that it shows which link is cheapest to move this year. Intervention points are political choices, not physical facts.

Institutional performance in fragile states depends less on formal authority than on whether mandates, money and information arrive together. Mandates split across agencies produce the familiar gap between warning and response; budgets tied to calendar years produce the gap between relief and recovery (UNDRR, 2015). Neither gap requires bad faith, only an ordinary division of labour. The strain is already visible in the numbers: ~20 million people are displaced by climate annually (UNHCR, 2023). Within security services and local government, the design question is where to place the authority that can move money and people within the same week. Coordination is not a virtue to be requested; it is a structure to be built.

Most disagreements about fragile states are not disagreements about facts. They are disagreements about weight: which risks count, over what horizon, and for whom (IPCC, 2022). That is why the same evidence can support both alarm and complacency without either side misreading the data. The scale involved is not marginal: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). As far as security services and local government is concerned, clarity about the weighting matters as much as clarity about the measurement. Stating the criterion in advance is what separates analysis from advocacy.

A recent marker points the same way: Cyclone Mocha (May 2023) displaced 1.7 million in Myanmar and Bangladesh (UN OCHA, WMO). Treated as a single observation it invites over-reading; treated as one of many it is informative. In the work of security services and local government, the report is a reminder that the response is being designed against a target that keeps moving.

Readers should know where this section stands on the evidence ladder. The direction of change is established; the pace is estimated; the local consequences are modelled. Statements that blur those levels are the ones most likely to mislead, and they are also the easiest to check. In the work of security services and local government, the operational reading is to record which limit applies to each decision before the decision is taken.


## 12.4 Food and Water Conflict

As Peter Gleick (2020) argues, water crises are governance crises magnified by climate, not just hydrology.

Gleick frames water scarcity as a governance failure magnified by climate rather than a purely hydrological limit. Allocation rules, pricing and infrastructure decide who is short. Drought turns a distribution problem into a crisis. In the terms of food and water conflict, the claim shifts attention from prediction to preparation.

Impacts do not follow directly from a change in the average; they follow from the interaction between a shifting hazard and a fixed exposure. A house built to a hundred-year flood standard meets a twenty-five-year event with alarming regularity once the distribution has moved (IPCC, 2022). Nothing about the house changed; the standard it was designed against did. The shift is quantifiable: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). For security services and local government, the lesson is that design standards have a shelf life and need a review date attached to them. An unrevised standard is a decision to accept a higher level of risk than the one originally debated.

Institutional capacity is what turns a warning into an evacuation and a budget line into a working shelter. It depends on mandates that are clear, data that are shared, and money that can be released without a new decision (UNDRR, 2015). Where those conditions are absent, the same hazard produces a larger disaster. The stakes are visible in the numbers: ~20 million people are displaced by climate annually (UNHCR, 2023). Within security services and local government, capability is better tested in a drill than in a document. A plan that has never been exercised is a hypothesis.

Discussion of food and water conflict usually begins as a technical question, and that framing hides the decisions underneath it. The technical part is real: measurement, modelling and attribution have improved sharply over two decades (IPCC, 2023). But the contested part is rarely the measurement; it is what the numbers imply for who acts, who pays and who is protected. One figure sets the scale: ~100 million people are affected by climate disasters annually (IFRC, 2022). Read that way, the question for security services and local government is not whether the trend is real but which choices remain defensible across the plausible range. A framing that treats uncertainty as a reason to wait gets the logic backwards: uncertainty is what makes early, reversible decisions valuable. As far as security services and local government is concerned, the practical question is what would have to change first.

The evidence base for food and water conflict is broad but uneven, and the unevenness is informative. Where many independent studies agree, confidence is high; where a single model family supplies the estimate, it is not (IPCC, 2021). One figure illustrates the state of knowledge: economic losses from climate disasters reach ~$200–300 billion per year (World Bank, 2022). The appropriate response to a strong signal with weak local detail is not to wait for precision but to design for the range. In the work of security services and local government, that means carrying uncertainty into the decision rather than resolving it beforehand. Plans that require certainty before acting are plans that act late.

The news cycle supplies a concrete marker: Typhoon Doksuri flooded Beijing in July 2023 with heaviest rain in 140 years, 1 million displaced (China CMA, Reuters). It is one line in a longer ledger, and the ledger is what matters. For security services and local government, what matters is not the record but the lead time it implies.

What the section establishes is narrower than a prediction and firmer than an opinion. It establishes that the direction is known, that the costs of inaction are concentrated on those least able to bear them, and that the available measures are known well enough to be tested. For security services and local government, the question is which of these decisions can be taken before the next event rather than during it.

## 12.5 Case Study: Conflict, Security and Stability

The case that anchors this chapter is documented and recent: Horn of Africa's 2022 failed rainy seasons pushed 22 million into acute food insecurity (FEWS NET, IPC). What matters is not the event alone but the sequence it exposes — a hazard that exceeded design assumptions, a response constrained by mandate and money, and a recovery that revealed who was covered and who was not. For security services and local government, the episode fixes the date at which the argument stops being hypothetical.

Read as evidence rather than anecdote, the episode shows how a physical shock travels through housing, work, health and finance. Damage does not stop where the water recedes; it continues through lost income, interrupted schooling and depleted savings — a sequence security services and local government can measure in its own records.

The test of any lesson drawn from the case is whether it changes a budget line, a standard or a protocol. If it changes none of the three, it remains a story.



## 12.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Conflict, Security and Stability

For conflict and security, the near term appears where rains fail repeatedly: by 2040 competition over water and grazing drives 20–30% more local disputes in the Sahel and Horn when drought lasts 2–3 years, and storm-driven outages add to civil unrest.

By 2050, food-price spikes and displacement pressure fragile states, and by 2100 a high-emissions path widens security gaps where institutions are already weak (IPCC, 2022; Mach et al., 2019).

Community water-peace committees, early-warning SMS, shared stockpiles, and transparent governance that shares resources before scarcity peaks reduce the trigger.

[[TABLE:Table 12.1 Future scenarios and predictions for climate change, conflict and security (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | local disputes +10–15% in dry years |
| 2050 | food-price spikes; fragile states stressed |
| 2100 | security gaps widen (high path) |

Watch local water-point conflicts, food-price protests, and displacement inflows across borders; climate's link to conflict is context-dependent and medium-confidence, strongest where governance is weak.
